INTRODUCTION 
 
I, the Chairman, Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Transport, 
Tourism and Culture, having been authorized by the Committee to present on its behalf, do 
hereby present this Three Hundred Fifty Seventh Rep ort on ‘Development of Niche Tourism 
(including Spiritual Tourism), Theme-based Tourist Circuits and Potential Tourist Spots’.  
2. The Committee heard the views of the representat ives of the Ministry of Tourism, on 
the subject, on 26 th  June, 2023. The Committee also heard the views of the representatives of 
the Federation of Hotel and Restaurant Associations  of India (FHRAI); Indian Association of 
Tour Operators (IATO); Indian Tourist Transporters Association (ITTA); and Adventure 
Tour Operators Association of India (ATOAI), in its  meeting on the subject, held on 18 th  
July, 2023. Further, the Committee heard the views of the representatives of the Ministry of 
External Affairs, on the subject, on 26 th  July, 2023. The Committee also took up the issue 
during its Study Visit to Mumbai and Goa from 5 th  to 8 th  July, 2023 and held discussions with 
the Ministry of Tourism; various Travel & Tour Oper ators; and State Governments of 
Maharashtra & Goa.  
3.  The Committee wishes to express its thanks to t he officers of the Ministry of Tourism; 
Ministry of External Affairs; State Governments of Maharashtra & Goa; FHRAI; IATO; 
ITTA; ATOAI; and various Travel & Tour Operators, f or placing before the Committee, the 
material and information desired in connection with  the subject and for clarifying the points 
raised by the Members. 
4. The Committee considered and adopted the Report in its meeting held on the 19
th  
September, 2023.   
 
 
(V. Vijayasai Reddy)

1 
 
REPORT 
 
A. INTRODUCTION 
1. Niche tourism is a specialised segment of the tourism market, where specific tourism 
products are tailored to meet the needs of a particular section/market segment.  As opposed to 
the more conventional mass tourism which is characterised by large numbers of tourists 
visiting popular holiday destinations  on standardised tour packages, niche tourism caters to 
small groups of people sharing the same, often uncommon interests, and provides curated and 
personalised tour packages to appeal to  the customers‟ interests.  While mass tourism is 
homogenous in nature with a standardised product for a large market segment , niche tourism 
is defined by its heterogeneous nature with unique products/services meant for small market 
segments.   
2. Niche Tourism is an all encompassing  term covering a wide range of special ised 
tourism products like cultural, medical, rural, adventure, wi ldlife, historical, gastronomic  
tourism, etc. These niches offer travellers the right avenues to immerse themselves in 
particular aspects that align with their innate passions.  Once considered expensive and elitist, 
niche tourism is now gaining popularity and is one of the fastest growing sectors in tourism. 
The growing popularity of niche tourism  can be largely attributed to ch anges in consumer 
behaviour as an increasing number of tourists seek immersive and more fulfilling travel 
experiences, the effect of Covid-19 pandemic which changed the preferences of the travelling 
public, the rise in awareness of sustainable tourism and such other factors.   
3. India has great potential for niche tourism being b lessed with diverse geography  
from mountains and forests to deserts and beaches, rich tangible and intangible culture with 
majestic palaces, forts and historical monuments, rich traditions of music and dance , yoga 
and Ayurveda. Modern India also showcases world class medical facilities and resources for 
conventions, meetings and exhibitions for the corporate world. In short, India offers a wide 
range of all-year-round tourism options with tourism products for all reasons and seasons and 
something for every kind of tourist. These undeniable advantages notwithstanding, both mass 
tourism and niche tourism have still vast unrealised potential.  Meanwhile, Thailand is poised 
to become the top golf destin ation in the world ; Nepal has a thriving mountain tourism ; and 
Bhutan has a successful Sustainable Tourism Strategy.  India is a land of spirituality being the 
birthplace and home  of se veral major religions. Despite being a land of immense spiritual 
significance, the full potential of Spiritual Tourism is yet to be realised in India.  
4. The Committee decided to examine the reasons why India has not been able to fully 
tap its potential for mass tourism and niche tourism when even its much smaller neighbo urs 
have successfully done so. The Committee also decided to study the impact of the Thematic 
Circuits launched under the Swadesh Darshan 1.0 , the PRASHAD Scheme on Spiritual 
Tourism and the Swadesh Darshan 2.0 with its emphasis on  destination tourism. Since ease 
of obtaining v isas is a major factor impacting  foreign tourist arrivals, t he Committee also 
heard the representatives of the  MEA on the subject of issuance  of visas and promotion of 

2 
 
Indian Tourism abroad as the Indian Missions have the sole charge of promoting India abroad 
after the closure of the Overseas Tourist Offices of the Ministry of Tourism.  
5. The first Meeting of the Committee was held on 26.06.2023 with  the Ministry of 
Tourism.  The Committee then heard the private Tour Operators and agencies, namely, The 
Federation of Hotel and Restaurant Associations of India ( FHRAI), Indian Tourist 
Transporters Association ( ITTA), Adventure Tour Operators Association of India ( ATOAI) 
and Indian Association of Tour Operators  (IATO) on 18.07.2023. The Committee also heard 
the views of the Ministry of External Affairs on 26.07.2023 on issuance of visa and 
promotion of Indian tourism abroad. 
 
B. NICHE TOURISM AREAS IDENTIFIED BY THE GOVERNMENT  
6. The Secretary, Ministry of Tourism , in her deposition before the Committee 
submitted that the following Niche Tourism Products are being currently promoted in India:- 
 
(i) Sustainable Tourism 
(ii) Rural Tourism 
(iii) Adventure Tourism 
(iv) Eco-tourism 
(v) MICE (Meetings, Incentives, Conferences & Exhibitions) and Wedding Tourism 
(vi) Cruise Tourism 
(vii) Medical and Wellness tourism 
(viii) Film Tourism 
(ix) Golf tourism 
(x) Spiritual Tourism 
 
7. New Niche tourism products may be added in due course as the Ministry takes the 
initiatives for identification, diversification, development, and promotion of niche tourism 
products to  overcome the aspect of „seasonality‟ and to  promote India as a perennial 365 
days‟ destination, to attract tourists with specific interest s, and to ensure repeat visits for the 
unique products in which India has a comparative advantage. 
8. The Ministry informed that the first step in development of all these Niche tourism 
areas was to outline a shared vision and roadmap in consultation with industry and State 
Governments.  The subsequent course of action involved institutionalising a support system 
for the Ministry,  establishing a mechanism for coordination with stakeholders and State 
Governments and lastly formulation of the project scheme and the allocation of the budget for 
development.  
9. In the first course of action, National Strategies and Roadmaps for d evelopment 
have been identified for Sustainable Tourism, Rural Tourism, Adventure Tourism, Eco -
Tourism, MICE  and Wedding Tourism , Cruise  Tourism, Medical and Wellness Tourism , 

3 
 
Film Tourism and Golf Tourism . Guidelines have also been formulated by the  Ministry to 
support Golf, Polo, Medical and Wellness Tourism . The details of the strate gic pillars in the 
National Strategies formulated for the Niche Tourism areas are placed at Annexure – I.     
10. The Indian Institute of Tourism & Travel Management (IITTM) has been designated 
as the Central Nodal Agency for Sustainable Tourism (CNA -ST), Rural Tourism & Rural 
Homestays, Adventure Tourism  and Eco-Tourism, for technical assistance and institutional 
support for implementation of the National Strategies. 
11. The Ministry also informed that it is jointly working with other Central Ministries on 
the Niche tourism areas which fall under their jurisdiction, like the Ministry of Environment, 
Forest & Climate Change for  Eco-Tourism, Ministry of Shipping, Ports and Waterways, for 
developing coastal areas/destinat ions under Cruise Tourism,  the Ministry of Health and 
Family Welfare Medical to promote Wellness Tourism and the Ministry of Information & 
Broadcasting for Film Tourism . The Ministry has undertaken convergence initiatives. 
Detailed areas of convergence are also being formulated by the Ministry of Tourism to guide 
effective convergence between line ministries/departments, states and private sector. 
12. Consultation with the Line Ministry was held on areas pertaining to Connectivity 
and Infrastructure, Skilling and Entrepreneurship, Tourism Product & Experiences.   
 
 
1. SUSTAINABLE TOURISM  
13. Sustainable Tourism is tourism that takes full account of its current and future 
economic, social and environmental impacts, and addresses the needs of visitors, the industry, 
and the  environment and host communities. The three basic principles of Sustainable 
Tourism are :- 
 Environmental Sustainability – Making optimal use of environmental resources and 
helping to conserve natural heritage and bio-diversity. 
 Socio-cultural Sustainability – Respecting the socio -cultural authenticity of host 
communities and conserving their built and living cultural heritage and traditional 
values. 
 Economic Sustainability – Ensuring viable, long -term economic operations, stable 
employment and social services to host communities and contributing to poverty 
alleviation.  
14. The Committee was informed that a  National Board for Sustainable Tourism and 
Eco-Tourism has been constituted under the Chairpersonship of  Secretary (Tourism) on 30 th 
June, 2022, comprising representatives from identified Central Ministries/organisations; State 
Governments/ UT Administrations and Industry Stakeholders. The Board shall guide the 

4 
 
operationalisation and implementation of various strategic initiatives as detailed below for 
development of sustainable tourism and eco-tourism in the country: 
(i) Detailed action plans and formulation of dedicated schemes  
(ii) Certification Schemes 
(iii) Capacity building, replication of national and global best practices  
(iv) Marketing and promotion 
(v) Private Sector Participation 
(vi) Destination and Product Development  
(vii) Specific strategies for sustainable and ecotourism 
(viii) Any other measures for growth of sustainable and ecotourism 
 
15. Three meetings of the Board have been organised till date. 
16. The Ministry apprised the Committee that sustainable tourism is not one part icular 
aspect of tourism but it  informs all segments of tourism, on how to make tourism more 
sustainable and responsible.  Since the formulation of the strategy, education and awareness 
have been the first course of action for which a National Summit and five regional workshops 
have been held.   The Travel for Li FE programme takes it to the next dimension of 
participation of the citizens or the tourist in promoting sustainable tourism.   
17. The Travel for LiFE Initiative  has been formulated on the philosophy of Mission 
Lifestyle for Environment (LiFE) which is a global mass movement against clim ate change 
led by Hon'ble Prime Minister, Shri Narendra Modi. The programme was launched during the 
third Tourism Working Group (TWG) meeting held in Srinagar, Jammu &  Kashmir. Travel 
for LiFE aims to promote sustainable tourism in the country in convergence with the National 
Strategy for Sustainable Tourism, through mindful and deliberate actions mobili sed toward 
tourists and tourism businesses in the consumption of tourism resources.  
 
18. Initiatives by the Ministry for promotion of Sustainable Tourism  
 Organisation of a  national summit for developing sustainable and responsible tourism 
destinations and Responsible Traveller Campaign in association with Responsible 
Tourism Society of India ( RTSOI) and United Nations Environment Programme 
(UNEP) on 4th June, 2022 at New Delhi on the eve of World Environment Day.  
 Five Regional Workshops organised in association with IITTM, UNEP and RTSOI to 
promote sustainable and responsible tourism. First workshop for States/ UTs of Central 
and Western region was organised on 29th November, 2022 at Khajuraho, M.P; Second 
workshop for States/ UTs of Eastern Region on 11 th January, 2 023 at Kolkata ; Third 
workshop for States /UTs of So uthern region at  Hyderabad on 28 th February, 2023; 
fourth workshop for States/ UTs of Northern region at Delhi on 22 nd March, 2023; and 

5 
 
the Fifth Regional Workshop for States/ UTs of North Eastern region at Guwahati on 
28th April, 2023.  
 In order to create mass awareness , the Ministry in collaboration wi th IITTM (Central 
Nodal Agency – Sustainable Tourism) releases a Sustainable Tourism Monthly 
Newsletter with a reach of 20,000 stakeholders of the tourism sector giving information 
about the best practices, change makers , and state initiatives tow ards sustainable 
tourism. 
 The Ministry of Tourism in collaboration with CNA -ST (IITTM) launched a Webinar 
series based on the pillars and principles identified in the National Strategy. The 
webinar consists of a panel of experts to discuss state -of-the-art topics in the tourism 
industry that can l ay down a clear path to achieving sustainability . It is organised on a 
fortnightly basis and is live -streamed on YouTube for the general public . So far four 
webinars have been successfully completed with a reach of about 3000 people. 
 Social Media handles in the name of “Sustainable Tourism India” have been created on 
the most popular and engaging social media platforms to sensitize the general public 
about the National Strategy, its core elements, and initiatives taken by the Ministry to 
implement the strategy. 
 The Ministry of Tourism had launched the Sustainable Tourism Criteria of India 
(STCI) for assessing and approving tourism enterprises on the basis of well -established 
and scientifically develop ed principles in 2014. Recently, the Ministry of Tourism in 
partnership with CNA -ST (IITTM) revised the criteria. It was re -established as a 
mechanism to recognize the tourism stakeholders that provide services based on 
principles of sustainability. The certification currently covers Accommodation Units, 
Tour Operators, and Destinati ons and is live  on Nidhi+ Portal. The first certification 
was completed recently with a Hotel in Odisha receiving a Silver Badge for its 
sustainability performance.  
 The Ministry is also looking into incentivizing „Green Tourism‟, which has been one of 
the major points of discussion in G -20 meetings. In the G -20 Tourism Working Group, 
the Ministry created the „Goa Road Map‟ giving recommendations for Sustainable and 
Responsible Tourism, best digitization practices of various countries, skill 
development, and tourism Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs).  
19. The Committee notes that India has be en ranked at 139, 134, and 128 in the 
years 2015, 2017 and 2019 , respectively  under Environmental Sustainability in  the 
World Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Ranking . While the new National Strategy 
and National Board for Sustainable Tourism have come into force in 2022, the Ministry 
had been promoting Sustainable Tourism in India for  nearly a decade after the launch 
of Sustainable To urism Criteria for India in 2014 . Not much progress has , however, 
been made in development of Sustainable Tourism in the last decade as substantiated by 
India‟s low rankings in this category . The Committee emphasizes that economic 
development should not be at the cost of the environment and recommends that the 
Ministry may ensure that the revised Sustainable Tourism Criteria  be scrupulously 

6 
 
implemented to minimize the adverse impact of tourism on the environment. While a 
Certification programme for tourism stakeholders, who have successfully implemented 
sustainable criteria, has been in existence for nearly a decade, only one Certification has 
been effected till date. The Committee desires to know the number of hotels surveyed 
for the Certification Programme and the strategy adopted for the survey. The 
Committee observes that while disbursement of monthly newsletters to stakeholders 
and workshops are useful for dissemination of information, the feedback from 
stakeholders like hoteliers and tour operators is equally important to comprehend the 
problems being faced while  implementing policies on the g round level. The Committee 
feels that the pace of the Ministry in promotion of sustainability in tourism is sluggish 
and recommends that the Ministry may step up its coordination efforts with both the 
stakeholders and the other Central Ministries for effective promotional campaigns.   
 
20. The Committee desires to know about the action taken on formulation of 
dedicated schemes, destination, and product development and other initiatives taken by 
the National Board for Promotion of Sustainable Tourism.     
 
2. RURAL TOURISM 
21. Rural Tourism is any form of tourism that showcases the rural life, art, culture, and 
heritage at rural locations, thereby benefiting the local community economically and socially as 
well as enabling interaction between the tourists and the locals for a mor e enriching tourism 
experience. 
22. The Committee was informed that a  Task Force for Development of Rural Tourism 
has been constituted  under the Chairpersonship of Secretary (Tourism) on 30 th June, 2022, 
comprising representatives from  identified Central Ministries/ organisations, State 
Governments/UT Administrations and Industry Stakeholders. The Task Force shall review 
progress on the implementation of the Strategy and coordinate action with all the 
stakeholders covering: 
 
(i) Preparation of detailed Action Plan for implementation of the Strategy for Rural 
Tourism and Rural Home stays 
(ii) Identifying national and global best practice and preparation of tool kits for their 
replication by the States and Rural Tourism Destinations  
(iii) Benchmarking State policies and standards and prepare model policies and minimum 
standards for rural tourism including B&B, Farm Stays, Home stays etc. 
(iv) Capacity Building measures including setting up of Resource Centres and Centres of 
Excellence  

7 
 
(v) Toolkits and guidelines for identification of clusters for rural tourism and preparation 
of development plans for the cluster, destination and product development  
(vi) Facilitating adoption of digital technologies for rural tourism 
(vii) Formulation of dedicated scheme to suppo rt development of rural tourism in the 
Country 
(viii) Private Sector Participation 
(ix) Any other measure, which can facilitate development of rural tourism in the Country. 
 
23. Two meetings of the Task Force have been organised till date. 
 
24. Initiatives by the Ministry for promotion of Rural Tourism 
 Rural Tourism India Website  - The Rural Tourism Village portal was launched on 
16th February, 2023. The website has information about the rural tourism destinations in 
India, rural homestays in India, Government and industry initiatives for rural tourism, etc.  
 Newsletter - The Ministry of Tourism in partnership with Central Nodal Agency  – 
Rural Tourism and Rural Homestays (IITTM) has come up with the Quarterly Newsletter of 
Rural Tourism India . The Newsletter will act as a communication tool to publicis e the 
initiative taken for the development of rural tourism in India. 
 Side event on Rural Tourism during the G20 meeting  - A side event on Rural 
Tourism for Community Empowerment and Poverty Alleviation on 07 th February, 2023 
during the 1st Tourism Working Group meeting held at Rann of Kutch. The event saw sharing 
of perspective by UNWTO on “Tourism and Rural Developm ent: Maximizing 
opportunities.” R ural tourism cases from India‟s regional governments, industry and 
community leaders, also showcased the potential of rural tourism in creating opportunities for 
livelihood and community empowerment. 
 Best Tourism Village Competition - The Ministry of Tourism, Government of India 
has launched the Best Tourism Village Competition on 16th February, 2023. The core aim of 
the competition is to develop the competitive feeling among  the villages of India which, in 
turn, will induce social and infrastructural development. Th e Best Tourism Villages 
recognised after the final evaluation will receive a national award from the Ministry of 
Tourism, Government of India. The villages will get featured on the Government of India's 
various online platforms. The first phase of competition i.e. the application of Best  Tourism 
Village Competition has been completed on 18th May, 2023. The competition received a total 
of 795 applications from 31 states and Union territories.  
 
 Developing Model Rural Tourism Clusters  - The Ministry of T ourism is working 
on the development of the model tourism village cluster development plan. A pilot study of 

8 
 
the Cluster Harshil – Bagori – Mukhwa – Dharali shall be undertaken. Also, the Ministry 
may engage an agency for planning and implementation from t he empanelled consultants for 
PDMCs and further explore funding opportunities under the Swadesh Darshan Scheme 2.0. 
This village cluster is located in the border area of the Uttarakhand. This project will be in 
convergence with the Vibrant Village Programme of the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA). 
25. The Committee is at a loss to note that the 2022 list of the United Nations World 
Tourism Organisation (UNWTO) featuring the 32 best tourism villages from 22 
countries,  does not contain a single village from India while several countries have two 
villages in the list, and Spain having even three villages. The Committee desires to be 
informed about the number of Indian villages which were nominated for the selection 
by the UNWTO.  The Committee notes that Khonoma in Nagaland has been selected for 
the “Upgrade” programme which would enable the village to receive the necessary 
support from the UNWTO to improve its conditions to meet the selection criteria.  The 
Committee wishes to know the e fforts being taken for the development of Khonoma 
after its inclusion in the upgrade list  of the UNWTO .  The Committee also desires to 
know as to how many Indian villages have so far been selected by the UNWTO in its 
best tourism villages list and the impact of such selection on the village tourism.  
26. The Committee observes that in a predominantly agrarian and rural economy 
like India, rural tourism has immense potential for boosting employment and income 
for villagers while offering opportunities to the city dwellers to take a break from the 
hectic pace of urban life to experience nature and local culture at a much slower pace. 
India is home to a diverse range of unique villages, each with its own cultural, 
architectural and historical significance. Unique villages such as „Mattur‟ in Karnataka 
where all the residents speak only Sanskrit and many others offer a glimpse into India‟s 
rich traditions and culture. The Committee, however, observes that the Rural Tourism 
website doe s not provide details as to how to travel to the unique villages nor details 
regarding rural homestays for most of the villages. Even the Khonoma village page does 
not provide any useful details for the tourists. The Committee recommends that the 
Ministry may ensure that the Rural Tourism website has all details of rural homestays, 
travel connectivity and tourist  spots in respect of every village featured for the 
convenience of the tourists.   
27. The Committee while appreciating the Best Village Competition initiated by the 
Ministry, is dismayed to note that in the past one year, only one Model Rural Cluster 
has been identified, without any action being taken on the ground. The Committee notes 
that the villages to be covered in the Vibrant Villages programme are located in far -
flung areas, and close to borders, often in difficult terrains. The Committee feels that 
the State of Uttarakhand has a number of border villages with rich traditions and local 
culture and recommends that the Ministry may make concerted efforts to develop the 
Model Rural Cluster in Uttarakhand within a targeted time schedule.   
 

9 
 
3. ADVENTURE TOURISM 
28. Adventure tourism is a type of niche tourism where exploration or travel involves 
some degree of risk (real or perceived) and may require special skills, and physical effort.  
Adventure tourism is very popular with the younger generation seeking experienti al travel 
that transcends traditional sightseeing to create meaningful and authentic experiences.   
 
29. National Board for Adventure Tourism  
30. The Committee was informed that a National Board for Adventure Tourism has been 
constituted under the Chairpersonship of Secretary (Tourism) on 30th June, 2022, comprising 
representatives from  identified Central Ministries/ organisations, State Governments/ UT 
Administrations and Industry Stakeholders. The Board shall guide the operationalisation and 
implementation of the strategy to promote and develop adventure tourism in the c ountry 
covering: 
(i) Detailed action plan and formulation of dedicated scheme  
(ii) Certification Scheme 
(iii) Safety guidelines  
(iv) Capacity building, replication of national and global best practices  
(v) Assessment of state policies and ranking 
(vi) Marketing and promotion 
(vii) Destination and product development 
(viii) Private sector participation 
(ix) Specific strategies for adventure tourism 
(x) Any other measures for growth of adventure tourism in the country. 
 
31. Four meetings of the Board have been organised so far. 
32. Other Initiatives by the Ministry for promotion of Adventure Tourism 
 Website of Adventure Tourism  - A website with the domain name 
adventure.tourism.gov.in is now live , consisting of document information, information 
related to vibrant villages, adventure tourism institutes and the latest efforts of Indian states 
towards adventure tourism. 
 Social Media Handles  - The Ministry in partnership with CNA -AT (IITTM) is 
operating social media handles for adventure tourism to promote adventure tourism of our 
country. Currently CNA -AT is having a ctive accounts on Twitter, LinkedIn, Facebook and 
Instagram. 
 
 Side Event on Adventure Tourism during G20 meeting  - A side event on 
Tourism in Mission Mode: Advantage Adventure Tourism was organised on 02nd April, 2023 

10 
 
during the 2 nd Tourism Working group Meeting at Siliguri with focus on North Eastern 
States. During the event , North Eastern States shared their current policies and future plans 
for adventure tourism. The Industry provided feedback on the same to help the States develop 
better strategies for promoting adventure tourism in the North East. 
 Draft Model Adventure Tou rism Law  - The Ministry of Tourism with CNA -AT 
(IITTM) has drafted t he Adventure Tourism Model law which covers the Obligations, 
Institutional Framework, Penalties, Registration and the Provision of Insurance. The Draft 
Model Adventure Tourism Law is a ref erence for the States which can be adopted with 
requisite modifications at their level. The draft has  been circulated to all States/ UTs for 
feedback. 
 Night Sea Kayaking Guidelines  - The Ministry of Tourism with CNA -AT (IITTM) 
has drafted Night Sea Kayaking  Guidelines which was further circulated to the States/ UTs. 
This document will help the States situated on Coastline to implement Night Sea Kayaking 
activity in a secured manner. The inputs received from the States/UTs are under examination. 
 Adventure Tourism Rescue Centers - The Ministry in consultation with CNA -AT 
(IITTM) has prepared  a draft concept of Adventur e Tourism Rescue Centre (ATRC) across 
the country to provide immediate response during incidents. The draft document has been 
circulated to the States and the Stakeholders for their comments and suggestions. CNA -AT is 
incorporating the suggestions received from the States and the stakeholders.  
 Mega Trails - The Ministry of Tourism is progressing towards the launch of 2 Mega 
Trails (Western Himalaya Trail and Ganga Trail) in the country. The industry stakeholders 
have submitted a detailed plan along with Terms of Reference.  
33. The Committee notes that there have been an increasing number of accidents in 
Adventure sports, especially in paragliding, which has claimed the lives of a number of 
tourists in the last few years. As per media reports , even “soft adventur e” sports like 
trekking have led to casualties in certain situations . The Committee has been informed 
by stakeholders that 3 Gs  - Guidelines, Guides and Gears  – are critical in risk 
mitigation. The Committee has also been informed that the Ministry has released 
guidelines for safety a nd quality norms for 15 Land Based, 07 Air Based and 07 Water 
Based adventure tourism activities, which cover the entire range of thrilling activities. 
Besides, some States have also adopted  various regulatory measures and safety 
protocols to address the inherent concerns of adventure tourism. ISO 21101:2014 and 
BIS have laid down safety management standards for compliance by adventure 
activities service providers that act as benchmarks for safe adv enture activities. The 
Committee desires to know about the impact of such safety guidelines on reduction of 
accidents in adventure tourism. The Ministry may give data on the number of accidents 
as also the casualties which have taken place in a dventure tourism in the country in  the 
last five years.  The Committee has also been informed that while testing and 
certification of  PPE (Personal Protectiv e Equipment) kits is necessary, it is difficult for 
small Indian manufacturing enterprises to obtain the universally accepted  international 

11 
 
certifications like  UIAA (French) (International Climbing and Mountaineering 
Federation). The Committee recommends that testing labs where certification is done 
by the BIS may help small enterprises in this field.   
34. The Committee feels that strict adherence to  safety guidelines and 
implementation of safety protocols can be possible only if responsible and licensed / 
certified adventure tour operators handle the activities. Licensed tour operators play an 
important role in promotion of adventure tourism as they conduct thorough risk 
assessments, implement safety measures, and provide high quality safety gears, besides 
engaging experienced and professional guides to handle emergency situations. The 
Committee has been informed that presently, adventure tour operators‟ licenses are 
issued by either the Centre or the States and there are still a lot of unlicensed tour 
operators in the country‟s adventure tourism sector who operate without requisite 
permits, certifications and regulatory approvals  to meet the requirements of safety, 
quality and also the law in the field. The Committee desires to know what action has 
been taken or is proposed to be taken by the Ministry on the unlicensed adventure tour 
operators. The Committee has also been informed  that the Ministry has circulated a 
draft model adventure tourism law to all the States/UTs for feedback. The Committee 
stresses that  high risk adventure sports cannot be regulated only by guidelines and 
recommends that the Ministry may make all efforts t o come out with laws soon so that 
only licensed and responsible operators who adhere to the  laid down  safety norms 
remain in business to provide adventure tourism services.  
35. The Committee has been informed that the government run institutions like 
Himalayan Mountaineering Institution in Darjeeling, the Nehru Institution of 
Mountaineering in Uttarkashi are booked two -three years in advance for a course in 
mountaineering or skiing or water sports. There is, therefore, a dearth of well trained 
guides in adventure sports . The Committee recommends that the Ministry should roll 
out more training programmes for guides and explore the possibility of establishing 
more institutions of international standards for adventure sports across the country.  
The Committee also feels that the training institutes are mostly located in North India 
with focus on high altitude mountaineering which is not so relevant for adventure sports 
in South India.  The Committee recommends that at least one or two Training In stitutes 
should be established in South India which focus on adventure sports suitable for its 
topography, altitude and climate.   
36. Regarding the standardization of safety gears, the Committee has been 
informed that presently, there is no s ingle system of certification of adventure 
equipments in our country . The Bureau  of Indian Standards (BIS)  is working towards 
setting standards for adventure equipment s that can demonstrate proven track record 
of reliability to help ensure a safe adventure experience. The Committee expresses its 
displeasure that India does not have standardized safety gears even for high risk 
adventure sports and recommends that the Ministry may expeditiously implement a 
uniform standard across the country for all adventure tourism verticals, prepared by 
Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS).  

12 
 
37. The Committee appreciates the initiative of Adventure Tourism Rescue Centre 
of the Min istry to provide for specialized facilities in emergency situations and urges 
that the Ministry may ensure that  medical treatment facilities are available near all 
adventure tourism locations.   
38. The Committee recommends that National I nsurance companies may also be 
encouraged to have policies covering adventure  tourism for licensed adventure tour 
operators, adventur e guides and the tourists  to provide support and promotion of  
adventure tourism.  
39. The Committee notes that since adventure tours are usually conducted in 
remote areas, adventure tourism has a significant impact on  economic development by 
way of income generation for local communities , increased business opportunities, and 
job creation. Besides, adventure tourism can empower local communities by involving 
them in tourism-related activities. At the same time, the Ministry should take proactive 
steps to determine the carrying capacity of popular adventu re tourism destinations to 
mitigate potential negative impacts such as environmental degradation and over  
tourism. The Committee observes that the Ministry should promote sustainabl e and 
responsible tourism management practices to harness the full development potential of 
adventure tourism.  
 
4. ECOTOURISM 
40. Ecotourism is responsible travel to natural areas that conserves the environment and 
improves the well-being of local people. The guiding principles for eco -tourism as laid down 
by the Ministry of Environment, Forests, and Climate Change are to adopt low impact 
wildlife tourism that protects the ecological integrity of forests and wildlife areas and builds 
environmental and cult ural awareness and respect . Use of indigenous, locally produced, and 
ecologically sustainable activities for tourism activities is also prescribed.   
 
41. National Board for Sustainable Tourism ( including subjects related to Eco -
Tourism) 
42. A National Board for S ustainable Tourism has been constituted under the 
Chairpersonship of Secretary (Tourism) on 30 th June, 2022, comprising representatives from 
identified Central Ministries/ organisations, State Governments/ UT Administrations and 
Industry Stakeholders. The Board guide s the operationalization and implementation of 
various strategic initiatives for development of s ustainable tourism and eco -tourism in the 
country. 
43. Three meetings of the Board have been held so far. 

13 
 
 Social Media Handles for Eco -Tourism - The Ministry of Tourism in partnership 
with CNA-ET (IITTM) has created Social Media Handles for promotion and propagation of 
Eco-Tourism activities. 
44. Side Event on Ecotourism during G20 meeting  - The Ministry of Tourism 
organised an event “Ecotourism as a vehicle for achieving Sustainable Development Goals” 
on 23rd, May, 2023 on the side lines of 3 rd Tourism Working Group meeting held at Srinagar 
to deliberate on various challenges and opportunities for developing Ecotourism in th e 
country following the principles of sustainability. The event provided an opportunity  to 
present India‟s efforts to develop and promote Ecotourism in the country. The event saw the 
participation of Line Ministries of Central Government, key States and Un ion Territories, 
Industry Stakeholders, national and international experts and academia.  
45. The Committee observes that guides and naturalists are one of the most 
important elements in imparting quality ecotourism experience by way of providing 
valuable educational, interpretive and experiential services to the visitors . The 
Committee has been informed that a Certification Scheme for tour operators and other 
service providers for ecotourism and adventure tourism is to be framed by the Ministry. 
The Committee desires to know whether the Scheme has been framed and the number 
of guides/naturalists who have been certified under this scheme so far.   
 
5. MEETINGS, INCENTIVES, CONFERENCES, AND EXHIBITIONS (MICE) & 
WEDDING TOURISM 
46. MICE is an acronym for Meetings, Incentives, Conferences and Exhibitions. The 
MICE industry also known as the “Meetings” or “Events” industry is a sub -segment of 
business travel but can also involve a  significant leisure component. MICE is a year round 
business and is beneficial for offsetting the lean seasons for leisure tourism. MICE is a high 
revenue-generating tourism as most delegates tend to opt for high end accommodations. It 
also creates huge employment opportunities in related hospitality service p roviding sectors 
like accommodation, food and beverage, convention services, transportation, tourism and 
entertainment. 
47. The Committee was informed about the  following i nitiatives by the Ministry for 
promotion of MICE Tourism. 
48. Formation of India MICE Board  - An India MICE Board  has been constituted  
under the Chairpersonship of the Secretary (T ourism), comprising representatives from 
identified Central Ministries/ organisations, State Governments/ UT Administrations and 
Industry Stakeholders. The Board shall coordinate implementation of various initiatives and 
strategies to facilitate growth of MICE Industry in India covering: 
(i) Detailed action plan and dedicated scheme for promotion of  MICE 
(ii) Institutional support for MICE  

14 
 
(iii) Developing Eco-system for MICE, replicating national and global best practices  
(iv) Enhance competitiveness of Indian MICE industry  
(v) Enhance ease of doing business for MICE events  
(vi) Marketing India as a MICE destination  
(vii) Skill development for MICE industry 
(viii) Any other measure, which can facilitate development of MICE Industry 
 
49. Two meetings of the Board have been held so far. 
 
 „Meet in India‟  – „Meet in India ,‟ a distinct sub -brand under „Incredible India‟ to 
promote the country as a MICE destination was launched during the Khajuraho MICE 
Roadshow. The sub brand will sharpen promotional campaigns to position India as an 
attractive MICE destination with all prere quisites – excellent connectivity, world class 
infrastructure, a knowledge hub and unique tourist attractions. 
 The Ministry of Tourism has recently launched a new Incredible India brand film on 
MICE Tourism. 
 The Ministry of Tourism is planning to launch a campaign shortly on MICE Tourism 
through Incredible India Digital Platform to showcase India as a preferred MICE 
Tourism destination globally.  
 E-Conference Visa has been i ntroduced for foreign visitors and the E-Visa scheme is 
now available to the nationals of 166 Countries.  
50. The Committee notes that India has less than 1% share in the Global MICE 
business despite being one of the fastest growing economies. India was ranked 28th with 
158 meetings in 2019 International Congress and Convention Association ( ICCA) 
ranking of countries. Indian cities are ranked poorly in the intern ational ranking of 
cities by ICCA in terms of the number of international associations meetings. There are 
only 07 cities of India in the top 475 cities,  the topmost being Delhi at the 75 th position 
with 37 meetings held and Ahmedabad at the bottom at the 424 th position with 04 
meetings. Even Goa, which is one of the country‟s top tourist destinations, is ranked at 
the 307th position with 08 meetings. The Committee also notes that India‟s outbound 
MICE market is one of the fastest growing markets globally.  
51. The Committee observes that a s per the National Stra tegy document on MICE, 
India‟s core MICE infrastructure and amenities are at par with most of the developed 
countries. The main reasons for the poor performance of India in MICE tourism is lack 
of focussed approach on MICE tourism, absence of system for capturing information 
about various national and international  MICE events and preparing for winning the 
bids,  high tax structure and costs and lack of standardization of processes and services. 
Besides, most of the successful international cities have their convention bureaus to 
market and provide support to the organisations and associations hosting event s 

15 
 
whereas there are no city convention bureaus in India.  Singapore, Thailand, UAE, 
China, Macau and Malaysia are already preferred destinations for MICE events in the 
region and India faces tough competition from these countries.     
52. The Committee notes that the Ministry has set the goals of enhancing India‟s share 
in the MICE business to 2% in five years from the current 1% and  India‟s ICCA ranking to 
the top 20, encourage the State Governments to set up 06 City level MICE Promotion 
Bureaus at major MICE destinations of Delhi, Mumbai, Bengaluru, Chennai, Kolkata and 
Goa, in the country in two years  and encourage the Convention Bureaus to bid for 
international events  so that  50% of the events coming to the country should be through 
MICE Bureaus. To achieve these targets,  the National Strategy for MICE, inter -alia, plans to 
set up a National Advisory Council for MICE under the Chairm anship of the Minister  of 
Tourism. The MICE Promotion Bureaus in every State and major cities would be the single 
point of contact for seeking information and facilitation for organizing MICE events.   
53. The Committee observes that  the Convention industry can enhance a city‟s 
reputation and visibility.  Creating a marke ting body to get more events will help, 
nurture and market the  MICE investmen ts in the city . The Committee recommends 
that the Ministry may make all efforts to ensure that such MICE Promotion Bure aus 
may be set up in the six identified cities within a targ eted time schedule to market and 
showcase the respective destinations as a favourable location for hosting business events 
and activities. 
54. The Committee observes that there are about 80,000 ,  04 star and 05 star hotel 
rooms in approved hotels, which for a country of the size of India, is quite insignificant 
(Annexure – II). The total number of rooms in the city of Bangkok is nearly three times 
than that of India. Adequacy of quality hotels can enhance the reputation of a 
destination as a suitable location for hosting MICE events, thus contributing to 
satisfaction of participants, repeat business besides word -of-mouth recommendation. 
Therefore, a dequate four and five star categories hotel rooms w ould have to be 
increased, for realising the goals of MICE tourism. The Committee recommends that 
the Ministry may assess the number of Convention Centres in the 04 and 05 star hotels 
of international stan dards in the country and determine the shortfall that would be 
required to host the targeted MICE events.  
55. The Committee also observes that connectivity, especially by air,  is one of the  
important aspect s that promotes  MICE tourism.  Since good international air 
connectivity reduces travel time and logistical challenges, t he Ministry should 
coordinate with the Ministry of Civil  Aviation to ensure that the identified cities of 
MICE destinations are well connected for international flights.   
  
6. WEDDING TOURISM 

16 
 
56. India has innumerable sites of historical, cultural, and aesthetic importance which can 
be leveraged for wedding tourism. The Committee was informed that a campaign  is being 
launched by the Ministry of Tourism to promote wedding tourism in India. The campaign 
will showcase spectacular and diverse locales, heritage, ethn ic diversity, profound rituals,  
well established wedding ecosystem, delectable cuisines and heart-warming hospitality, to 
invite both domestic and international clientele to create lasting memories. 
57. The Committee has been informed that one crore weddings take place in India 
every year and the number is bound to increase in the coming years. The Committee 
observes that Destination Weddings with events and celebrations spread out over many 
days can offer several benefits to the local economy and local ecosystem. But, India‟s 
huge potential for wedding tourism has not been exploited.  The Committee appreciates 
that Kerala Tourism has launched a music video, “Shubha Mangalyam”  in 
collaboration with a Youtuber and singer, for promotion o f wedding tourism in Kerala 
which has garnered much popularity.   The Committee recommends that similar efforts 
may be made by other states to promote destination wedding tourism.  
58. The Committee recommends that apart from development of infrastructure  and 
facilities to support the growth of wedding tourism, the Ministry may also facilitate easy 
and uniform process of registration of marriages in every state of India even for couples 
belonging to other states. 
 
7. CRUISE TOURISM 
59. Cruise Shipping is one of the most dynamic and fastest growing components of the 
leisure industry worldwide.  India with its vast and beautiful coastline, virgin forests and 
undisturbed idyllic islands, rich historical and cultural heritage, can be a fabulous tourist 
destination for cruise tourists. With the Indian economy developing at a steady pace, middle 
class growing in number and increasingly possessing disposa l incomes which could be spent 
on leisure activities, Indians could also take on cruise shipping in a big w ay. However, in 
2022, it was estimated  that the global cruise Tourism market was around $ 8 bn and the 
Indian market was estimated at around $100 mn which was an estimated share of around 1%, 
despite all the competitive advantages India has globally in cruise tourism. 
60. The Committee has been informed that the  National strategy for Cruise Tourism shall 
be finalised after detailed stakeholder consultation.   
61. The Committee was informed about the following initiatives taken by the Ministry of 
Tourism for promotion of Cruise Tourism in India -  
 The Ministry of Tourism provides Central Financial Assistance (CFA) to the State 
Governments/ UT Administrations/ Central Government Agencies for development of 
Tourism including Cruise Touris m and Cruising along rivers, under the Scheme for 
„Assistance to Central Agencies for Tourism Infrastructure Development.‟  

17 
 
 The coastline of the country and its inland waterways have enormous potential to 
develop cruise tourism, both international and domestic. To harness this, a Task Force 
has been constituted with Secretary (Tourism) as the Chairman and Secretary 
(Shipping) as the Co -chairman. The Task Force consists of representatives from the 
ports, Ministries of Health, Home Affairs, External Affairs, Customs, CISF, Coastal 
States, etc. The 14th Meeting of the Task Force was held on 03rd May, 2023.  
 The Ministry of Tourism organised the event “Cruise Tourism” on the side lines of 4 th 
Tourism Working Group Meeting under India‟s G20 Presidency on 19 th June, 2023. 
The event deliberated on the strategies to promote Cruise Tourism as a Model for 
Sustainable and Responsible Travel and see the participation of G20 member 
countries, invited countries and industry stakeholders.  
62. The Committee observes that prior to Covid, there was a 35 per cent year -on-
year growth of cruise traffic in t he country. P ost-Covid, from 2021 onwards, there has 
been an increase in cruise traffic but the pre-Covid growth has still not been achieved. 
Prior to Covid, there were 4.7 l akh passengers on cruises and now, about 3 lakh 
passengers have been on cruises.  
63. The Committee also observes that the Cruise Shipping Policy was first 
formulated by the Ministry in 2008 when the industry was  in its infancy in India. The 
Policy aimed, inte r-alia, to increase the number of cruise passengers by 10 lakh by the 
year 2010, well developed rail, road and air connectivity and promotion of cruise 
vessels. Customs duty on bunkering for cruise and coastal ships, then at 33%, was cited 
as a factor towards making cruise operations uncompetitive. As part of conducive fiscal 
regime measures this issue was resolved by the Department of Revenue vide Notification 
No. 31/2014 dated November 11, 2014 by exempting Customs and Excise duty leviable 
on bunker fuels. Some progress has definitely been made in cruise tourism inasmuch as 
India now has its own luxury cruise liners . The first International Cruise Vessel from 
Chennai to Sri Lanka has recently been flagged off in June, 2023. India is , however, still 
way behind achieving the target of 10 lakh passengers as even in 2023, not even half the 
target has been achieved . The statistics on cruise vessels at some of the Major Ports 
provided by the Ministry show that cruise tourism has a long way to go to catch up with 
the pre-Covid numbers. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should 
endeavour to calibrate the strategy for Cruise tourism by addressing the challenges like 
inadequate infrastructure, lack of proper facilities at ports, environmental concerns due 
to waste disposal and pollution, etc. in a time -bound manner, besides  giving incentives 
to cruise companies to come to Indian ports.     
64. The Committee has been informed about five projects sanctioned under the 
Assistance to Central Agencies Sc heme for promotion of Cruise Tourism in Kerala 
(Annexure- III) from 2016 -17 to 2019 -20.   The Committee desires to know if similar 
assistance has been given for promotion of Cruise Tourism in other States.   
 

18 
 
8. MEDICAL AND WELLNESS TOURISM 
65. Medical tourism  is travel for treatment for  curative purposes. Wellness tourism is 
about travelling for the primary purpose of achieving, promoting and maintaining maximum 
health and a sense of well -being and though often clubbed with medical tourism, the two 
types operate in separate domains and meet different consumer needs. India has been ranked 
10th in Medical Tourism Index (MTI) for 2020 -21 out of 46 destinations of the world by 
Medical Tourism Association. As per estimates by Global Wellness Tourism, India ranked 7th 
position in 2017 with 56 million trips in wellness tourism and total revenue of USD 16.3 
Billion. 
66. The Committee was informed about the following initiatives taken by the Ministry 
of Tourism for promotion of Medical Tourism in India. 
67. The Ministry of Tourism constituted the National Medical & Wellness Tourism 
Board (NMWTB) with the Hon‟ble Minister (Tourism) as its Chairman in 2015 to provide a 
dedicated institutional framework to take forwa rd the cause of promotion of Medical and 
Wellness Tourism including the Indian system of medicine covered by Ayurveda, Yoga, 
Unani, Siddha and Homeopathy (AYUSH).   ITDC is the Nodal Agency for medical and 
wellness tourism.  
 
 Medical Visa – „Medical Visa‟ category has been introduced, which can be given for 
specific purpose to foreign travellers coming to India for medical treatment. „E - Medical 
Visa‟ and „E -Medical Attendant Visa‟ have also been introduced. The Ministry of Home 
Affairs vide its OM dated 15.03.2022 restored e -Tourist Visa with immediate effect  for 
nationals of 156 countries. 
 Wellness Tourism - Most of the hotels/resorts are coming up with Ayurveda Centers. 
The leading tour operators have included Ayurveda in their information brochures to attract 
the tourists. 
68. The steps taken by the Government to promote medical/ health tourism include 
promotion in overseas markets through roadshows, participation in travel marts, and 
production of brochures, CDs, films, and other publicity mat erial. Health tourism has been 
specifically promoted at various international platforms such as World Travel Mart, London, 
and ITB, Berlin. Yoga/ Ayurveda/ Wellness are being promoted in the past years in the print, 
electronic, internet and outdoor medium under the Ministry of Tourism‟s “Incredible India 
Campaign.” 
69. Celebration of International Day of Yoga - The Ministry of Tourism conducts 
International Day of Yoga (IDY) on 21st June every year for promotion of wellness Tourism . 
It is celebrated all across India with the active participation of Travel Trade, Yuva Tourism 
clubs, Hospitality Industry, Tourists etc. Activities were conducted in order to raise awareness 
of the many benefits of practicing yoga. 

19 
 
70. The Committee was informed that India is a sought after medical tourism destination 
because of state-of-the-art medical facilities, high quality healthcare professionals, relatively low 
cost of quality treatment as compared to western countries and no waiting time for treatment.   
India also has a unique advantage of offering Ayurveda, Yoga & Naturopathy and other 
traditional systems of medicine for the treatment of various ailments . In order to highlight all 
the strengths of the country, India will be marketed as a medical and wellness tourism 
destination under the “Heal in India” brand.  
71. However, the medical tourism potential remains unrealised because there is lack of 
government regulations for the medical travel sector  which means that quality of services 
cannot be monitored. India maintains a robust ac creditation system through the National 
Accreditation Board for Hospitals and Healthcare Providers (NABH). There is , however, not 
much awareness about NABH in foreign countries and the international patient s still attach 
much greater value to JCI accredita tion. India still has relatively a smaller number of JCI 
accredited hospitals. Unavailability of Medical Insurance Portability and non -coverage of 
traditional systems of medicine under insurance policies at the international level impacts the 
choice of India as Medical Value Travel (MVT) destination. 
 
72. The Committee observes that lack of regulation on quality of service is a major 
concern and appreciates that the National Strategy is to encourage registration of all 
medical tourism facilitators with the Government. The Committee, however, 
recommends that such registration should be mandatory as standardisation of services 
is the biggest impediment affecting med ical tourism in India. The Committee 
recommends that while NABH accreditation may be highlighted in various source 
markets, the Ministry should encourage major Indian hospitals to obtain the JCI 
accreditation.  
73. The Committee also observes that till now, no  significant efforts or investments 
have been made to build an ecosystem around hospitals. Guesthouses and patient 
specific hotels need to be built around hospitals.   
74. The Committee also recommends that the Ministry may explore the 
opportunities in cross border telemedicine as India has sufficient skilled personnel to 
become a global hub in this regard. The Committee also recommends that the Ministry 
may promote the traditional medicines of India like Ayurveda on an equal footing.  
 
9. FILM TOURISM 
75. The Committee has been informed that the National strategy for Film Tourism shall 
be finalised after detailed stakeholder consultation.   
76. In order to leverage the power of Film Tourism to promote destinations, an event on 
“Promoting Incredible India through Film Tourism” was organ ised on 22nd May 2023 on the 

20 
 
sidelines of the third Tourism Working Group meeting held at Srinagar. The event provided 
an opportunity to the States/ UTs to share their policies and best practices which helped them 
develop film tourism. The event also provided an opportunity to the Industry s takeholders to 
give their suggestions and feedback on the factors that will encourage filmmakers to shoot in 
various locations in the country. Deliberations on national strategies  required to promote film 
tourism both for domestic and foreign films were also made. 
77. The Committee observes that films can have a massive im pact on destination 
marketing as visitors are often inspired to visit filming locations featured in popular 
films. The impact is such that the annual tourist influx is said to have increased by 40 % 
in New Zealand, the site of the movie trilogy of “Lord of the Rings ” after the release of 
the films. In India, the release of the film "3 Idiots" led to a significant increase in 
tourist arrivals in Ladakh to visit the Pangong Lake featured in the film. India produces 
the highest n umber of films in the world and film tourism has significant potential for 
growth in India. Many Indian films , especially those regarding per iod dramas,  
showcase historical places and characters and can be useful to highlight the history 
behind the tourist places. The Tamil movie “Ponniyin Selvan” based on one of the 
greatest novels in Tamil literature, is a fictional account of the historical character of 
Raja Raja Chola, the Chola king who built the Brihadeeswarar Temple in Thanjavur, 
one of the biggest temples of India. The Chola dynasty was famous for building temples, 
many of which are now World Heritage Sites. The Committee notes that the Tamil 
Nadu Tourism Development Corporation has announced a “Chola Tourism Circuit” 
covering the Chola Temples and the Veeranam Lake, also built by the Cholas. Tourism 
potential of such historical and religious sites can be harnessed through the medium of 
films. The Committee feels that while India has the advantage of producing the highest 
number of films in the world which also in a way depict the innate  historical 
significance of the places of tourism interests, the Ministry has not paid adequate 
attention to the great potentia l of films to showcase India. The Committee recommends 
that the Ministry should endeavour to devise the strategy to fully tap into the benefits of 
film tourism.   
 
10. GOLF TOURISM 
78. The Ministry of Tourism has framed guidelines for extending financial support for 
promotion of Golf Tourism. The Ministry invites Expressions of Interest (EOI) from the Golf 
Clubs, Golf Event Managers, State Governments/ UT Administrations, Approved Tour 
Operators/ Approved Travel Agents and Corporate Houses seeking financial support from the 
Ministry of Tourism for Golf Events, Golf Shows, Golf Promotional Workshops/ Events/ 
Annual Meetings/ Seminars eligible  for financial support, with a view to promote Golf 
Tourism to and/or within India under Incredible India brand as well as to provide an 
opportunity for these events to use the association with the Incredible India brand to achieve 
international standards. Applications received through EOI are evaluated by the India Golf 
Tourism Committee (IGTC) headed by Secretary (T) in its meetings held from time to time. 

21 
 
79. Presently the Ministry is in the process of reviewing these guidelines and is profiling 
lesser known Golf Tourism destinations for promotion. 
80. The Committee observes that Golf was introduced to India by the British and 
in 1829, the first golfing establishment, the Royal Calcutta Golf Club, was established . 
In contrast, Thailand‟s first professional golf was established only in 1924. Yet Thailand 
has emerged as  the world‟s top golfing destination while golf tourism is yet to take 
shape in India . In fact, many Indians visit Thailand as golf tourists . The Committee 
observes that India has much of the same landscapes and climatological conditions as 
Thailand, like a sunny climate all year around in most places which makes winter golf 
possible. Yet, India has not taken any special initiative to  attract both domestic and 
international golf enthusiasts . Thailand has promoted a Thai Golf Pass which is a 
unique loyalty program me exclusive to Thailand providing a seamless golf experience . 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry should come up with initiatives such as 
developing world -class golf infrastructure, p romoting tournament s, and providing 
holistic tourism experience to the visiting golfers in the country. 
81. During its Study Tour in Kerala, the Committee had been informed tha t an 
amount of Rs. 2464.99 lakh  had been sanctioned to Sports Authority of India under the 
Scheme “Assistance to Central Agencies for Tourism Infrastructure Development”  in 
Kerala for upgradation of the Golf Course at the 165 year old SAI Trivandrum Golf 
Club under the  Sports Authority . The Committee observes that the project was 
sanctioned in 2016 -17 but as per media reports, the upgradation seems to have been 
taken up only in 2020-21.  The Committee further observes that only half the sanctioned 
amount has been released and the status of the project is stated to b e “Project Closed”.  
The Committee desires to know the reasons behind this when in reality, the project is 
not completed. The Committee also desires to know whether other Golf clubs in the 
country have been given similar assistance under the Scheme.  
82. The Committee observes that the Ministry is yet to take significant action on 
Golf Tourism, Eco -tourism, Film Tourism, and Wedding Tourism while taking some 
measures to promote  Sustainable Tourism, Rural Tourism, MICE and Medical 
Tourism, and Cruise Tourism. A plethora of wildlife sanctuaries and tiger reserves exist 
in India yet not much attention seems to have been paid to Wildlife Tourism. Kerala has 
shown the way for Caravan Tourism  with the establishment of one Caravan Park and  
proposal for  3 new Caravan Parks, 10 Caravans and subsidy of Rs. 7.5 lakhs for 
Caravan owners. Despite the successful efforts of Kerala towards promotion of Caravan 
Tourism, the Ministry has not endeavored to replicate the same in other states . The 
Committee also appr eciates the efforts of Kerala Tourism to develop the “Muziris 
Heritage Circuit”, the largest Heritage Conservation Project in India which would 
reinstate the historical and cultural signif icance of the legendary port of  Muziris and 
also the Alappuzha Heritage Circuit which includes the development of the defunct port 
of Alappuzha into a marina . The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
explore the potential of Casino Tourism, Culinary Tourism, and Polo tourism, the last 
being believed to have originated in Manipur. The Committee also observes that in the 

22 
 
aftermath of the pandemic “Remote Working” has gained traction. Due to the rise in 
online work, workers often move to locations even in other countries which offer a 
lower cost of living or scenic locations , etc. and continue to stay for a long period of 
time. India offers all modern facilities required for such remote working,  like good 
internet facilities, a low cost of living compared to Western countries and many  serene 
and beautiful plac es for living . The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
explore the possibilities of such niche tourism experience.  
83. The Committee further observes that though the Ministry has drafted the 
National Strategies and constituted National Boards for most areas of Niche Tourism, 
developed web sites and published newsletters yet, coordination with the concerned 
Ministries and formulation of project schemes und er the various niche areas seem to be 
wanting. The pace of implementation on the ground appears slugg ish. Most of the 
National Boards and Task Forces have held only 2 -3 meetings in a year . Most of the 
websites for Niche Tourism do not provide complete and detailed information , 
necessary for the tourists . The Ministry‟s interactions with the stakeholders a nd State 
Governments appear  sporadic. The growing gap between the strategic vision and 
implementation needs to be bridged and the Committee recommends that the Ministry 
may take necessary action for the same. 
84. The Committee observes that while the Ministry‟s tourism policies stress the 
importance of attracting private investment and developing Public Private Partnerships 
for development of tourist infrastructure and facilities, the Ministry has not given any 
information on PPP projects in any scheme, be it niche tourism, thematic circuits or 
destination tourism . The National Strategy on Eco -Tourism has PPP partnerships as 
one of its strategic components. The Committee has come to know from the website of 
the Department of Economic Affairs regarding PPP projects that Eco -tourism projects 
have been proposed in Andaman & Nicobar Islands by Ministry of Home Affairs 
(MHA). The Committee desires to know the status of these projects and whether the  
Ministry of Tourism proposes to develop similar projects under Eco -tourism and other 
niche tourism areas and Swadesh Darshan 2.0 on PPP basis . The Committee directs the 
Ministry to submit details of PPP projects taken in the various schemes so far . The 
Committee observes that private participation in product development or marketing 
and promotion in tourism can be valuable and recommends that the Ministry may  
endeavour to bring in private investments in its various tourism products and schemes.  
85. The Commit tee observes that most of the tourists in India come from four 
source countries of US, Canada, Bangladesh and UK which account for nearly 60%  of 
the inbound tourists. India has only a small percentage of tourists from Europe, Africa, 
Central and South Amer icas. The tourist inflow from West Asian countries is also very 
low though the number of outbound tourists from India to these countries is much 
higher. The Ministry has informed the Committee  that improving connectivity with 
other regions especially direc t flight connectivity, tapping the potential of medical 
tourism, promoting India as a hub for MICE tourism , especially after the inauguration 
of world -class convention centre at Pragati Maidan, creating better infrastructure for 

23 
 
eco-tourism, adventure tour ism, religious tourism circuits, emphasizing on cleanliness 
and security, etc. are some of the steps that can be taken to increase inbound tourism in 
India. The Committee acknowledges that synergy among the various Central Sector 
Schemes of different Minis tries is essential for promotion of   tourism. To achieve this, 
inter-Ministerial coordin ation is of crucial importance. The Committee has been 
informed by the stakeholders that consultations between the Ministry of Tourism, other 
Ministries and the associations take place in a fragmented m anner and there is no 
proper coordination and consultations among Ministries involving matters of tourism in 
the country. The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Tourism may step up 
efforts for coordination with other concerned Ministries for tourism promotion.  
86. The Committee stresses that connectivity to places especially to places of niche 
tourism which are often in far flung areas is of utmost importance in  the direction of 
promoting tourism in the country . The Committee has been informed by stakeholders 
that even places like Aurangabad, which as part of the Golden Triangle, used to be well 
connected to as many as eight cities is now connected to only two places. The Committee 
desires to know from the Ministry, the reason behind this drastic measure. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry may pay special attention to the air 
connectivity of tourist places being promoted as also of the existing popular places  of 
tourism interest.   
87. The Committee has been informed by  the stakeholders that the present level of 
training and skilling is not adequate as niche tourism demands high level of training 
and skilling to meet the specific requirements and expectations of the tourists pertaining 
to that sector of niche tourism . The Committee recommends that the tourism sector 
needs to undertake reskilling and upscaling of its manpower by way of imparting them 
skill upgradation capacity building training in different niche areas from time to time.  
88. The Committee has been informed that availability of good quality hotel 
accommodation is currently very low in India as compared to other leading countries having 
tourism potential. India has less than 0.2 million cla ssified rooms which may  have a 
correlation to our global tourism share which is around 1%. In comparison , China‟s global 
tourism share is estimated to be above 5% and they have an estimated more than 4 million 
hotel rooms. The USA is estimated to have more than 6 m illion hotel r ooms and their 
international tourism share is estimated to be above 7%. The Committee observes that out 
of the 1,291 approved hotels in India only 565 hotels belong to Three Star and below 
categories and out of the 1,00 ,980 hotels rooms only 18,225 rooms belong to these 
categories which is grossly inadequate to meet  the tourism numbers targeted by the 
Ministry. The Committee also observe s that tourists, especially in n iche tourism, come 
from all strata of society and the hotel accommodation should cater to all  categories of 
tourists. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should incentivise those in the 
hospitality business to make concerted efforts  to ensure the availability of sufficient 
number of hotel rooms of all categories for the optimal growth of tourism sector.   

24 
 
89. The Committee further observes that creation of hotel infrastructure would involve a 
mammoth capital investment.   The Committee has been informed that as return on 
investment in the hospitality sector takes at least 15 years, availability of long term 
loans becomes a problem.  The Committee recommends that grant of infrastructure 
status to the hotel industry may be examined on priority as loans with longer repayment 
schedule and suitable interest rates would be available for creation of hotel 
infrastructure.  The Committee has been informed that though industry status has been 
accorded to hotels by many state Governments, but the incentives and privileges 
associated with the industry status have not been conferred to the hospitality sector.  
Granting Industry status to the tourism and hospitality sector would entitle the sector 
across all states to pay various taxes and tar iffs on industrial rates instead of on 
commercial rates presently.  The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
consider grant of Industry status to the tourism and hospitality sector by way of a 
uniform central notification as it would enhance the over all competitiveness of the 
tourism industry.   
90. The Committee has been informed by the stakeholders that Tourist transport 
vehicles are treated on par with trucks and other commercial vehicles due to which the 
speed limit is restricted for tourist vehicles whereas private cars have  higher speed 
limits and faster movement.  In areas of niche tourism like MICE and luxury tourism, 
high-end tourists do not enjoy the rides especially in long road journeys and as such, the 
speed restrictions cause avoidable annoyance in the tourist experience. The Committee 
has learnt from the media reports that the Government is planning to review the speed 
limits and recommends that the Tourist transport vehicles may not be treated in the 
same category as other commercial vehicles  and the speed limits may suitably be 
revised/increased. Another minor but significant point raised by the stakeholders was 
that curtains should be allowed in tourist buses as tourists, especially  the foreigners, are 
not used to tropical weather, especially the heat of Indian summers. The Committee 
observes that tourism promotion is as much about the wholesome journey experience as 
it is about the destinations and recommends that the Ministry may consider the issue.   
91. The Committee has been informed by  the stakeholders that high tax rates in 
India in comparison to neighb ouring countries (Annexure – IV) is affecting tourist s‟ 
arrival in India. The Committee observes that in 2019, the GST rates had been reduced 
for hotel room from 28% to 18% for hotel rooms with tariffs of Rs. 7500/ - and above, 
18% to 12% for tariffs of less than Rs. 7500/ - and none for tariffs of less than Rs. 1000.  
The Committee appreciates the initiative of the Ministry of Finance and recommends 
that the Ministry of Tourism may examine the impact of the 2019 reduction in GST on 
tourist arrivals and appropriately take up with the Ministry of Finance for  further 
reduction in GST, if need be, for the targeted growth of tourism sector.   
92. The Committee observes that under the Service Exports from India Scheme 
(SEIS), the Government offered domestic exports duty credit scrips in the range of 5% 
to 7% of the net foreign exchange earned depending upon the nature of services.  These 
SEIS scrips  could be used to pay import duty or can be encashed by selling it to any 

25 
 
importer.  The SEIS Scheme had not been extended beyond 2019 -20.  While examining 
the Demand for Grants 2023 -24, the Committee had recommended that the Ministry 
take up the SEIS issu e with the Ministry of Commerce to increase the rate of SEIS 
benefits to 10% and extend the scheme for a further period of 2 -3 years.  The 
Committee reiterates its earlier recommendation as even after 02 years after the 
pandemic, the tourism sector is yet to redeem its pre-pandemic position.  
 
11. SPIRITUAL TOURISM  
93. India is the land of many religions and faiths since ages and these have immensely 
contributed to the development of Indian heritage values. Religion and spirituality are 
common motivations for travel, with major tourist destinations having developed large ly as a 
result of their connections to sacred places, persons, and events. The growth of domestic 
tourism in India largely depends on pilgrimage tourism.  
94. Spiritual Circuits under Swadesh Darshan Scheme:- 
 The Ministry of Tourism launched „Swadesh Darshan ‟ Scheme in 2014 -15 and 
provided financial assistance to the State Governments/ UTs Administration/Central 
Agencies for development of tourism infrastructure at various destinations. Under the 
scheme 15 thematic circuits have been identified which also incl udes Krishna Circuit, 
Buddhist Circuit, Spiritual Circuit, Ramayana Circuit and Tirthankara Circuit.  
 Under the Spiritual theme i.e. Buddhist Circuit, Krishna Circuit, Ramayana Circuit, 
Spiritual Circuit and Tirthankara Circuit, 23 projects are sanctioned in various 
States/UTs for an amount of ₹ 1365.01 Cr. out of which the Ministry has released an 
amount of ₹ 1185.00 Cr. till date. List of projects sanctioned under the Spiritual theme 
of Swadesh Darshan is at Annexure – V. 
 The Ministry of Tourism is promot ing destinations related to spiritual tourism through 
the portals – incredibleindia.org and utsav.gov.in to celebrate the diverse faiths and 
belief systems of the country.   Spiritual events and festivals are showcased along with 
live darshan of various religious places through Utsav Portal. Also, destinations 
associated with spiritual tourism are amplified through the Social Media handles of  the 
Ministry of Tourism. 
 The Ministry of Tourism extensively promotes spiritual tourism, as one of the 
important tourism products of the country in print and  electronic media as also in their 
respective social media handles such as Facebook, Twitter , etc. Religious places, 
pilgrimage centers, etc. are being included in the Tourist Itinerary and recommended to 
both international and domestic tourists. 
 Recently, the Ministry and various State Tourism Departments attended World Travel 
Market (WTM)  2022 in London, Feria Internacional del Turismo  (FITUR) 2023 in 
Madrid and ITB 2023 in Berlin, Arabian Travel Market (ATM ) 2023, Dubai. The 

26 
 
Ministry of Tourism promoted and showcased varied tourism products 
of India including Spiritual Tourism through India pavilions. 
95. The Committee notes that of 23 Spiritual Circuits, 05 are yet to be completed 
out of which 02 circuits of Kera la are lagging much behind schedule. The work on the 
Sabarimala-Erumeli-Pampa-Sannidhanam circuit in Pathanamthitta district in Kerala 
had been initiated in 2016 -17 while the  Sivagiri Sree Narayana Guru Ashram circuit 
had been initiated in 2018 -19. At the time of the Demands for Grants 2023 -24, the 
Committee had been informed that the reason for delays in these two Kerala Circuits 
was due to lack of clearance from the Temple A uthorities. The Committee has been 
informed during its Study Visit to Kerala in September, 2023 that the status of physical 
completion of the two projects is 76% and 51% , respectively. The Committee is 
disappointed to see the slow progress  on these importa nt projects which have a target 
completion date of June, 2023 and October, 2023 respectively . The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry should develop a clear cut Standard Operating 
Procedure (SOP) and obtain prior clearances and approvals from relevant authorities 
for future compliance in the interest of timely completion of projects.  
96. The Committee notes that the Buddhist circuit has been fully developed but as 
can be seen from the Tourism Statistics 2022, the numbers of foreign tourists from 
predominantly Buddhist countries has not shown any significant uptick. The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry may focus on promotion of Buddhist C ircuit in these 
countries and desires to know the number of events conducted by the Indian Missions 
there, showcasing  India‟s Buddhist circuit. The Committee recommends Audio -Visual 
Guides that offer information about Buddhist sites and monuments. 
97. The Committee has been informed that Bodh  Gaya, one of the most important 
sites in the Buddhist circuit, does not have good connectivity with the rest of the places 
in the Buddhist circuit. The Committee calls upon  the Ministry to examine the 
connectivity issue of Bodh Gaya and focus on improving the overall connectivity of all 
the Spiritual Circuits for increasing tourist footfall. 
 
C. PRASHAD SCHEME  
98. The “National Mission on Pilgrimage Rejuvenation and Spiritual, Heritage 
Augmentation Drive (PRASHAD)” was launched by the Ministry of Tourism as a Central 
Sector Scheme with the objective of integrated development of identified pilgrimage/heritage 
destinations. The scheme aims at creation of pilgrimage/spiritual tourism infrastructure 
development at the identified destinations. Details of the project s sanctioned under this 
scheme are placed at Annexure – VI.  
99. The Committee observes that at the time of  the Demands for Grants, 2023 -24, 
the Committee had been informed that 20 out of 45 projects under this scheme had been
