completed, 09 projects were ongoing and 16 projects were yet to be developed.  The 

27 
 
Committee observes that the status remains almost the same as only  21 projects have 
been completed. Most of the reasons for delay given by the Ministry earlier were due to 
lack of appro vals from the ASI, temple authorities, forest clearance , etc. Upon further 
query by the Committee, the Ministry has now given the general reasons for delay that 
projects are usually sanctioned at Brownfield locations and well -known pilgrimage/heritage 
destinations with heavy footfall and hence , pose a challenge for carrying out construction 
activities during the peak visit hours at the destination. In a few instances, delays have been 
encountered due to cert ain unavoidable factors. These factors include religious and local 
issues, challenges faced in hilly areas or difficult terrains, and adverse weather conditions or 
natural calamities that affect project execution, etc. The period of the pandemic (March, 2020 
to January, 2022) also had an adverse impact on the project execution and performance of the 
Scheme. Despite these occasional delays, a significant number of projects, 21 in total, have 
been successfully completed, and an additional 05 projects are near ing completion. The 
Ministry strives to address issues related to religious beliefs and local stakeholder s concerns 
in order to minimize delays and ensure the smooth completion of the project. 
100. The Committee observes that the PRASHAD scheme was launched in 2014-15 
and completion of 21 projects out of 45 is certainly not a significant achievement , even 
taking into account the Covid years. The Committee points out that spiritual circuit 
projects were taken under Swadesh Darshan Scheme 1.0 also and the Ministry has had 
adequate experience of the kind of problems that ordinarily come up while dealing with 
construction and development  at pilgrimage sites.  The Committee calls upon the 
Ministry to be mindful of the potential problems and calibrate suitable response at the 
DPR stage, so that the projects are not held up at later stages , causing both time and 
cost overrun.   
101. Upon being asked about the steps taken to complete the projects under this scheme, 
the Ministry has informed that it is closely following up with respective State & UT 
Governments for timely completion of approved projects. The Ministry of Tourism had done 
midterm amendments in scheme guidelines and changed the funding pattern. As per the 
guidelines, the first instalment shall be disbursed post subm ission of tender documents by the 
State Governments/UTs. The Ministry of Tourism also conducts regular site visits and 
follow-up meetings with the implementing agencies to monitor the progress of projects. 
Monthly Progress Reports from State /UT Governments are evaluated to track the progress of 
the scheme and identify any areas where there are delays or challenges. The Ministry of 
Tourism conducts routine virtual meetings with implementing agencies to discuss the 
progress of the projects, identify any challenges or issues, and provide advice and guidance to 
the State Government so that projects are implemented in a transparent and accountable 
manner. The Ministry is also working on a dedicated online PRASHAD Dashboard so that 
timely updates of the projects can be tracked and monitored. The dashboard will provide real-
time updates on project status, including financial information, physical progress, and 
implementation challenges. 
102. The Committee appreciates that the Ministry has taken the initiative to amend 
guidelines and funding pattern midterm where necessary and recommends that the 

28 
 
Ministry may revise the timeline of the delayed projects and  ensure that the projects 
under this sc heme are completed within the revised timeline . The Committee also 
recommends that the PRASHAD Dashboard when online may be  monitored on a real 
time basis at the level of a senior functionary in the Ministry to ensure the completion of 
all pending projects.  
 
D. THEME-BASED TOURIST CIRCUITS – SWADESH DARSHAN 1.0 AND 2.0 
SCHEMES 
103. The mission of the Swadesh Darshan Scheme was to create a robust framework for 
integrated development of tourism destinations in partnership with the States/ UTs and local 
governments for promoting sustainable and responsible tourism in the country. Under the 
Scheme, 15 thematic circuits have been identified which also includes Spiritual Circuits i.e. 
Krishna Circuit, Buddhist Circuit, Spiritual Circuit, Ramayana Circuit and Tirthankara 
Circuit. Theme -based Tourist Circuits are the circuits around themes su ch as spiritual, 
culture, ecology, niche, etc. A theme-based circuit can be related to a state or region covering 
more than one State or Union territory. The 76 projects under Swadesh Darshan had multiple 
destinations, and covering 900 Tier-II and Tier-III cities.   
104. The Committee was informed that the „Swadesh Darshan ‟ Scheme was launched in 
2014-15 and the Ministry provided financial assistance to the State Governments/ UTs 
Administration/ Central Agencies for development of tourism infrastructure at vario us 
destinations.   
105. Since the inception of  the Scheme, 76 projects  have been sanctioned in 31 
States/UTs for an amount of ₹ 5292.57 crore. Out of these, 63 projects are completed till date 
and remaining projects are scheduled to be completed by December, 2023. 
106. Under the said Scheme, basic amenities and various tourism products were 
developed across various destinations such Convention Centres, Log Huts, Cafeteria, Public 
Facilities, Tourist Facilitation Centres, Souvenir shops, Cultural Centres, Interpret ation 
Centre, Last Mile Connectivity, Provision of Ramps at public places, Adventure Activities, 
Facade beautification, Landscaping works (Hard & Soft), Parking, Signage etc.  Six 
Convention Centers have been created under „Swadesh Darshan‟ 1.0

E. PROMOTION OF SWADESH DARSHAN SCHEMES 
109. The Committee was informed that the State Governments/UT Administrations play a 
key role in identifying and developing the theme -based tourism circuits in their respective 
states. The Ministry of Tourism through its various initiatives supports the States 
Governments to develop and promote these theme -based tourism circuits. Further, the 
Ministry of Tourism provides financial assistance to the States/UTs through its major 
schemes for development of tourism infrastructure at various destinations such as Swadesh 
Darshan, PRASHAD, etc.  
110. The Ministry of Tourism promotes tourist destinations including theme-based tourist 
destinations through its website www.incredibleindia.org, Social Media platforms and other 
promotional material such as digital creatives, films , etc. Further, the Ministry of Tourism 
provides Central Financial Assistance (CFA) upto Rs.50.00 Lakh to State Governments & 
UT Administrations on the proposal of fairs/festival and tourism related events (i.e. seminars, 
conclaves, conventions, etc.). 
 
111. Utsav Portal  - The Utsav Portal website ( www.utsav.gov.in), a digital initiative 
launched by the Ministry of Tourism, aims at showcasing all the events, festivals and live 
darshans across India to promote different regions of the country as popular tourist 
destinations worldwide. The objective is to showcase var ious events and festivals of India on 
a global platform and increase tourism awareness, attractions, and opportunities by providing 
tourists with contextual digital experiences. 
112. The Ministry has showcased 1118 Events and 55 Live Darshans  on the Utsav 
Portal website till date. 

30 
 
113. Recent Initiatives:- 
 The IRCTC has launched Baba Saheb Ambedkar Yatra by Bharat Gaurav Tourist 
Train on the recommendation of the Mi nistry of Tourism. The 7 nights / 8 days tour 
was launched on the auspicious occasion of 132 nd Birth Anniversary of Dr. Baba 
Saheb Ambedkar in 2023. The Yatra includes visit  to prominent places associated 
with the life of Baba Saheb Ambedkar like New Delhi, Mhow, Nagpur and sacred 
Buddhist sites like Sanchi, Sarnath, Gaya, Rajgir and Nalanda. 
 The Ministry of Tourism organ ised a Familiarization (FAM) Trip on Bharat Gaurav 
Tourist train tour on Ambedkar Circuit to promote and popularize this special 
initiative.  
 The Committee was also informed that the Ministry of Tourism regularly reviews the 
physical progress of the project at various levels. The delay in completion of project 
has occurred due to various reasons including clearance, local issues, etc. A statement 
containing project -wise issues of various ongoing projects as informed by State is 
given at Annexure – VIII. It may be noted that no escalation in project cost is 
noticed and all the projects are with in the original sanction cost . The Ministry of 
Tourism regularly holds review mee ting with officials of State/UT Government for 
tackling various issues, addressing bottlenecks and ensuring speedy completion of the 
project. 
114. The Committee notes that at the time of the Demand for Grants 2023 -24, the 
Ministry had informed that 17 projects of Swadesh Darshan 1.0 were pending  for 
completion. Four more projects have been completed and now 13 projects are targeted 
for completion by December, 2023. The Committee notes that out of the remaining 13 
projects, only 04 are showing some progress and the rest are still at the same stage as 
they were in Marc h, 2023. The Committee further notes that 02 projects are delayed 
due to court case/arbitration, 04 due to lack of clearance from forest/temple authorities 
and the remaining 03 due to delays in procurement of cruise boats and floating 
restaurants, contract or issues and inclement weather and difficult terrain (in case of 
J&K). The Committee feels that the lack of progress in these projects in the past few 
months does not augur well for their timely completion by December, 2023. The 
Committee also notes that most of these projects began in 2016 -17 and are already 
much behind their schedule. The Committee also notes that the four projects which are 
delayed due to lack of clearances from forest or temple authorities  are at the final stage 
of completion. The Committee desires to know why the clearances have not been 
obtained at the time of the finalisation of the DPR and are pending even at the 
culmination of the project. The Committee recommends the Ministry to make  every 
effort to complete the remaining 13 projects by December 2023 as the projects have 
already been considerably delayed. The Committee also desires the Ministry to  monitor 
the real time progress of the projects at the level of a senior functionary and e nsure 
their completion without any further delay.  

31 
 
115. The Committee appreciates the Utsav Portal website and the Tourist Train 
initiatives of the Ministry. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should study 
the impact of the projects completed under the Swadesh Darshan 1.0 scheme on the 
tourism and analyse the shortcomings so that the same could be improved in SD 2.0.   
 
F. SWADESH DARSHAN 2.0 
116. The Secretary , Ministry of Tourism,  in her deposition before the Committee 
informed that no new projects were sanctioned under the Swadesh Darshan Scheme 1.0 after 
2018-19. After a very high level and detailed review the  Swadesh Darshan Scheme was 
revamped to ensure that the funding under t he Scheme remains more impactful an d visible. 
Even while the Theme -based Circuits remain relevant, it was felt that keeping in view the 
resources, only 40 -50 destinations be focused on in the Swadesh Darshan 2.0 Scheme. The 
following areas were identified for improvement in the Second Phase of the Scheme: 
(i) Broad-basing of Central Sanctioning and Monitoring Committee 
(ii) State-level Institutional Structure for the Scheme 
(iii) Strategic selection of the destinations 
(iv) Detailed benchmarking and gap analysis of destination 
(v) Focus on both hard and soft Interventions 
(vi) Strengthening of Project Implementation and Monitoring 
(vii) Operations and maintenance on sustainable basis 
(viii) Promotion & marketing of the destinations 
(ix) Impact Assessment 
 
117. Swadesh Darshan 2.0 brings about a major shift in  the scheme to make it a holistic 
mission to develop sustainable and responsible tourism destinations.  The key principles to 
be followed under Swadesh Darshan Scheme 2.0 are:- 
(i) Develop benchmarks and standards for major tourism themes 
(ii) Sustainable and responsible tourism 
(iii) Destination and tourist-centric approach 
(iv) Integrated development of tourism destination 
(v) Focus on Domestic Tourism 
(vi) Policy and Institutional Reforms 
(vii) Operation and maintenance on sustainable basis 
(viii) Synergy with other central and state schemes 
 
118. Following major themes have been identified for tourism under the Scheme:- 
(i) Culture and Heritage Tourism 
(ii) Adventure Tourism 
(iii) Eco-Tourism 
(iv) Wellness Tourism 
(v) MICE Tourism 

32 
 
(vi) Rural Tourism 
(vii) Beach Tourism 
(viii) Cruises – Ocean & Inland 
 
119. As per statutory requirement, 10% of the allocation for Schemes has to be earmarked 
for the development of the North-Eastern Region. Accordingly, the Ministry has provided an  
allocation of Rs. 229.00 crore  under BE 2023 -2024. Under the Swadesh Darshan 2.0,  12 
destinations have already been identified in North -Eastern States including Sikkim with an  
average outlay of Rs. 50 crore . Out of total budget allocation of Rs 1412 crore for Swadesh 
Darshan scheme, Rs. 98.50 crore is earmarked specifically for Tribal Sub-Plan for creation of 
tourism infrastructure in the tribal areas. 
120. When enquired by the Committee on the criteria of selec tion of 55 destinations 
under Swadesh Darshan Scheme  2.0, the Ministry informed that the States had been 
consulted on the same and a format had been circulated to the States for preparing the 
State Perspective Plan . The Ministry further informed that there are two principles 
involved in selection of a destination that states which have not been represented should 
find a place in the list and places with immense potential should also be included . The 
Committee recommended that a wide consultation process should be initiated before the 
selection of any such destinations as they have a great impact on the local economy.  
121. Upon observation of the Committee that 50 destinations had also been 
announced in the Union budget speech, 2023, the Ministry confirmed that these 50 
destinations were over and above the 55 dest inations under the Swadesh Darshan 2.0 
and would be selected in challenge mode. Both the State Governments and the 
Stakeholders at the Chintan Shivir meeting had been asked for feedback and the 
selection process would be fairly competitive . The Committee emphasised that selection 
of destinations should be a consultative process so that there is an equitable distribution 
of destinations in the country across sectors.   
122. The Committee observes the differences ( Annexure – IX) in  funding pattern, 
disbursal of funds and receipt of Utilisation Certificates in the Swadesh Darshan 2.0 
Scheme, and appreciates the new features of Impact Assessment of the projects under 
the Scheme and monitoring of the Scheme through a Comprehensive Porta l. The 
Committee, however, observes that all the themes of the Swadesh Darshan 1.0 Scheme 
have been repeated in the 2.0 Scheme with no new additions. The claim of the Ministry 
to focus on fewer destinations in the second phase of the Scheme has also been d iluted 
by the addition of 50 more destinations.   
123. Upon query , the Ministry has replied that as per  guidelines, the scheme will 
encourage development of benchmarks and standards for generic and theme specific 
development of tourism destinations and the Stat es will follow the benchmarks and standards 
while planning and developing the projects. While major  themes have been identified in the 
guidelines under the scheme, all other themes will fall in one of the major themes and a 
destination may be developed for one or more major themes. 

33 
 
124. The Committee observes that there does not appear to be a clear cut distinction 
between the two phases of the Swadesh Darshan Scheme as the same theme based 
strategy seems to  have guided the implementa tion of the second phase also. The 
Committee is also not satisfied with the reply of the Ministry that all themes evolved in 
future will fall in one of the major themes as the basic idea of n iche tourism is that it 
provides a particular experience which cannot be obtained in other forms of tourism.  
Regarding benchmarks and standards, the Committee fails to understand why the same 
were not provided in the earlier phase of the scheme. The Commi ttee feels that the 
Ministry has in the past decade alternated between theme -based tourism and 
destination based tourism and has endeavoured to incorporate elements of both in all 
the programmes that it has formulated. The Committee recommends that the Ministry 
may ensure that the guidelines issued to the states are unambiguous in the kind of 
destinations and themes to be developed. 
125. The Committee observes that given the immense potential of niche tourism in India, 
there is a need to create capacity and ensure skilling of employees for various segments of 
niche tourism. Specially trained manpower is needed to cater to the growing demand for 
niche tourism especially among foreigners. The Committee wishes to know from the 
Ministry, the steps being taken to increase the capacity and availability of skilled manpower 
in the niche tourism sector. 
126. The Committee has been informed that the Ministry of Tou rism through the 
Capacity Building For Service Providers (CBSP) scheme is taking steps for capacity building 
in hospitality, travel and tourism sector by skilling the newly inducted persons and re-skilling 
and upskilling the already employed persons at tourist destinations. However, for catering to 
the growing demand for niche tourism , especially among foreigners, the challenge could be 
to identify the youth interested in learning various foreign languages and also the trained 
trainers in various languag es. The Ministry h as entrusted IITTMs to conduct l inguistic 
programs, all over the country. Other challenge s being faced while working on c apacity 
building and skilling in niche tourism areas include lack of Data of Master Trainers, lack of 
awareness among  the community, lack of industry support, inadequate community 
involvement, etc. 
127. The Indian Institute of Tourism and Travel Management, an autonomous body under 
the Ministry of Tourism, Government of India through its five Centres and two camp offices 
is d eveloping the skilled manpower to strengthen the Indian Tourism Industry. IITTM has 
established courses for the tourism sector to train and certify manpower to strengthen tourism 
industry. IITTM offers MBA and BBA courses in Tourism, conducts guide trainin g 
programme and short-term skill development training programme. 
128. The National Institute of Water sports , a centre of IITTM, under the Ministry of 
Tourism, Govt. of India offers various tailor -made Water based adventure and also certifies 
the persons. 

34 
 
129. The Committee appreciates the initiatives taken by the Ministry to prepare a 
skilled workforce for catering to the  increasing demands of the tourism industry for its  
anticipated growth.  The Committee desires to know the number of students in the 
tourism cours es at IITTM, the number of trained guides and whether the numbers 
would be adequate to meet future demand.  
130. The Committee appreciates that the Ministry has taken the digital initiative of 
the Incredible India Tourist Facilitator (IITF) Online Programme to enable people to 
learn about tourism at their pace, earn Certificates as Facilitators , and become part of 
the tourism industry. The Committee has been informed that the Facilitators certified 
under the IITF scheme are not allowed to enter the monuments as a result of which 
their employment prospects are affected. Tour Operators hesitate to employ Facilitators 
as they would also have to separately employ guides to go into the Monuments with the 
tourists. The Committee desires to be informed of the status in this regard and 
recommends that the Tourist Facilitators may be imparted training  in all aspects of 
tourism so that they may act as Guides inside the monuments as well . The Committee 
also observes that in places like Serengeti  National Park in Tanzania, the drivers of the 
Safari Vehicles are well trained to act as guides to the Park . The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry may explore the possibility of multi -skilling of the 
Tourist Facilitators so that they may also perform  the roles of Drivers and Gu ides, 
depending on the requirements.  
 
G. POTENTIAL TOURIST SPOTS 
131. The Ministry informed that  the States/UTs play a key role in identifying and 
developing the potential tourist spots in their respective states. The Ministry of Tourism 
through its various initiatives supports the State/Union Territory Governments to promote 
various t ourism spots through marketing and  promotion, destination development, skill 
development, etc.  The Ministry of Tourism also conducts media campaigns on various 
platforms including Online and Social Media to promote the potential tourist spots.  
132. Further, the Ministry of Tourism organises various tourism related events such as 
Bharat Parv, Paryatan Parv, International Tourism Mart, etc and participates in various events 
such as Tra vel Fairs/Exhibition for tourism promotion and marketing. The Ministry of 
Tourism also extends financial assistances to States/UTs for Fairs, Festivals & Events.  
133. The Ministry of Tourism through its various schemes such as Swadesh Darshan, 
PRASHAD and C entral Financial Assistance (C FA) to Central Agencies provides financial 
assistance to State Governments / UTs /Central Agencies for developing tourism 
infrastructure at various tourist destinations.  
134. In the meetings with the stakeholders, the Committee was in formed about 
various potential tourist spots in the country which hav e been placed at Annexure – X. 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry may examine the concept of Greenfield 

35 
 
Tourism where new locations can be developed as potential tourism spots with the 
participation of Government of India and the concerned State Government with private 
investments for creation of modern tourist infrastructure, hotels and all tourist facilities 
and connectivity by air, rail and road . The Statue of Unity near Kevadia in Gujarat is a 
classic example of Greenfield Tourism, being a man -made tourist destination which has 
earned a formidable reputation as a popular tourism spot . Another example is tourism 
on the sand dunes near the Salasar Balaji Temple in the Churu Dis trict of Rajasthan 
where hotels and restaurants have come up to cater to the tourists who visit the temple 
and stay to enjoy the sand dunes in the desert . The Committee observes that 
development of such Greenfield tourist spots would take the pressure off the already 
established tourist spots which sometimes suffer from the adverse effects of 
overtourism.     
135. The Committee recommends that the Ministry may while promoting 
destinations in Arunachal Pradesh or Lakshadweep or such areas which require special 
permits, highlight the requirement in its campaigns so that tourists are aware of the 
rules and are able to plan their schedule accordingly.  
136. The Committee further observes that the Ministry had formulated the Draft 
National Tourism Policy in July, 2022 incorporating key recommendations of NITI 
Aayog, Sectoral Group of Secretaries, global best practices from organizations such as 
UNWTO and polic ies and strategies formulated by other countries after multiple 
rounds of consultations and feedback from Central Ministries, State Goverments and 
Industry Stakeholders.  More than a year later, the policy, however, has still not been 
finalized.  The Commi ttee recommends that the Ministry may make every effort to 
ensure that all concerns of the stakeholders in the tourism and hospitality sector are 
addressed and the National Tourism Policy is put in place in a time-bound manner.  
 
H. PROMOTION OF TOURISM ABROAD BY INDIAN MISSIONS 
137. The Committee was informed that the Ministry of External Affairs has been playing 
a key role in promoting Indian Tourism abroad through its collective efforts along with its 
Missions/Posts abroad. Indian Missions have been closely working with the Overseas Tourist 
Offices before their closure on 31st March 2023. 
138. In October 2021, the Tourism Officers have been designated in 20 Indian Missions 
abroad (Australia, Canada, China, France, Germany Italy, Japan, Malaysia, Myanmar, Oman, 
Portugal, Russia, Singapore South Korea, Spain, Sri Lanka, Thailand, The Netherlands, UK 
and USA) to promot e tourism. The Tourism Officers in 20 Missions are IFS Officers in the 
ranks of Second Secretary, First Secretary, or Counsellor. All 20 Tourism Officers have held 
detailed discussions with the Ministry of Tourism in March , 2022 wherein they presented 
country specific strategy papers on promotion of tourism to India for their respective 
countries.  

36 
 
139. The Indian Missions maintain a database of the local tour -operators and agencies 
and remain in regular touch with them. Sometimes, they also purchase tickets for  Indian 
Technical and Economic Cooperation  (ITEC) scholars proceeding to India through tour 
operators. Information exchange with tour operators/agencies is promoted through 
meetings/emails/mutual discussions. Missions also consult with seve ral state governments to 
organise familiaris ation visits and awareness building about tourism among foreign tour 
operators. Tour operators are invited by the Missions to participate in information awareness 
and state tourism promotion programmes. Missions send the details  of Indian tourism 
including promotional material, website links for Incredible India, Utsav India, famous tourist 
places, etc. regularly to the operators. In addition, WhatsApp groups have been formed for  
faster and efficient communication. 
140. The MEA does not have a separate earmarked budget for tourism promotion but the 
Ministry does so under the budget for overall trade and cultural promotion under three types 
of funds: 
a) States Facilitation Abroad Fund  – The MEA coordinates and facilitates the efforts of 
the States and Union Territories in the areas of trade, investment, culture and tourism, 
with the help of Indian Missions/ Posts abroad through States Facilitation Abroad 
Fund. The Missions/Posts abroad may ut ilize the funds allotted, inter -alia, for 
promotion of Tourism (inbound) of States/Union Territories. The details of  funds 
allocated to the Missions/ Posts under the head -States Facilitation Abroad during last 
three years are enclosed at Annexure – XI (Table 1). 
b) Publicity Fund - This is managed by MEA‟s External Publicity and Public Diplomacy 
Division and is used for promotion of tourism and culture abroad. The details of funds 
allocated to the Missions/ Posts under the head -„Publicity‟ during the last three years 
are enclosed at Annexure – XI (Table 2). 
c) Market Expansion Fund - To enable our Missions/Posts abroad to effectively respond 
to the growing demands of Indian industry and business, the MEA disburses funds for 
“Market Expansion Activities ”. Missions/Posts abroad may utilize this fund, inter -
alia, for promotion of Tourism. Details are included at Annexure – XI (Table 3). 
141. Several initiatives undertaken by the Missions/Posts in this regard are as follows: 
 Tourism Road shows and Know India Seminars: Missions and Posts regularly organize 
tourism Road shows and „Know India‟ seminars in their host countries both for the 
Ministry of Tourism, Go vernment of India,  as well as State -led initiatives. These help 
in popularizing Indian tourism, connect ing the tour operators of both sides for tie ups 
and garnering interest in outreach among the travel industry in host countries. 
 Incredible India Campaign: Missions have been actively working for the promotion of 
Incredible India Campaign. This is being do ne by distribution of Incredible India 
brochures translated in several languages; and organizing Incredible India road shows. 
The Missions have also been organizing Travel to India campaigns under which 

37 
 
promotion of Incredible India is being done through b illboards at prominent locations 
and advertisements on local city buses plying on busy routes. 
 Social Media Outreach : Missions also undertake promotion of Incredible India 
campaign though their social media channels, which has found resonance in the last 
few years. These efforts have been complemented by online competitions. 
 Participating in Travel Expos : Missions have been participating in global and regional 
travel markets and travel expos to promote tourism in India. 
 Tourism as a part of commercial and a cademic events: Missions also promote India for 
its plethora of tourist attractions across themes during the programs/engagements at all 
platforms – Universities, Think -tanks, Business and Commerce Chambers and 
interactions thereof.  
 Promotion Material : Mi ssions have also been working to publish and distribute 
promotional material positioning India as a favourite Tourist destination. Missions also 
work for the promotion of State tourism through distribution of publicity material 
during their various Commerc ial events, state days and seminars. Missions also 
promote tourism through slide shows and posters, LED screens and billboards at the 
prominent places. 
 Medical Tourism:  Missions have been organis ing several outreach seminars, 
exhibitions, and investors‟ summits to promote medical tourism in India, and AYUSH 
systems of medicine and related wellness therapies. Missions have organ ised AYUSH 
promotion campaigns among travel and tourism industry operators, medical industry 
and among spas/resorts/wellness centers. 
 Signing several Memorandum of Understanding  for Promotion of Tourism with 
Foreign Governments 
 Inviting Artists to India and organize their events in association with ICCR. 
 Providing „Grants in Aid‟ to local artists/groups  who promotes Indian Culture, Dances, 
etc. 
142. Other activities include:  
 Special Day/  Weeks for promotion of tourism such as Celebration of India Day, 
Constitution Day, Gandhi Jayanti, International Yoga Day, etc. 
 Celebrating Indian Festivals - Promoting Indian Culture and Cuisine (Food Festival) 
 Exhibition 
 Indian Film Festival- Screening Indian Movies/Biopic of Freedom Fighters 
 Setting up of India Corners in several Universities, Institutions, Art Galleries, Indian 
Missions/Posts 
 Participating in local festivals for promotion of India tourism 

38 
 
 
143. On a query by the Committee regarding the number of MOUs signed with foreign 
governments for promotion of Indian tourism and the impact of such MO Us, the Ministry 
informed that t he signing of Agreemen ts / Memorandum of Understanding (MoUs) with 
various countries is an ongoing process and the Ministry of Tourism has signed Agreements/  
MoUs in the field of tourism cooperation with other countries and multilateral fora. The ma in 
objective of such Agreemen ts/ MoUs signed by the Ministry of Tourism is primarily to 
promote tourism between both sides. The importa nt components of the Agreements / MoUs 
include the following: 
(i) Exchange of information and data related to tourism. 
(ii) Cooperation between tourism stakeholders including hotels & Tour operators. 
(iii) Exchange of visits of Tour Operators/Media/Opinion Makers for promotion of two way 
tourism 
(iv) Exchange programmes for cooperation in Human Resource Development. 
(v) Investment in the Tourism and Hospitality sector. 
(vi) Exchange of experience in the areas of promotion, marketing, destination development 
and management. 
(vii) Participation in travel fairs/exhibitions in each other‟s country 
 
144. The det ails of countries for tourism Mo Us signed with foreign cooperation are 
placed at Annexure – XII. 
 
145. The MEA has created 3Ts Dashboard (Trade, Tourism, and Technology). Indian 
Missions are uploading data on the dashboard, as and when available to them. Our Missions 
continue to make efforts to increase bilateral trade, enhance inbound tourism and make 
available advanced and critical technology to India 
146. The Committee observes that there were 10.93 million Foreign Tourist Arrivals 
(FTAs) in India during pre -pandemic year 2019, but the current FTA numbers are still 
well below the pre -pandemic levels . In one of the studies by the National Council of 
Applied Economic Research (NCAER), it is estimated that tourism in India would not 
regain its pre -pandemic levels till 2026 . In this scenario, promotion and marketing of 
tourist destinations and products, especially by the Indian Missions abroad, assumes 
importance if India is to regain its pre-pandemic tourism numbers.  
147. The Committee had been informed by the stakeholders that  currently no tourism 
promotion is being conducted overseas. When enquired by the Committee, the MEA gave the 
details regarding events and campaigns organ ised by the MEA after the closure of the 
Overseas Tourism Offices.  The MEA also informed that response to such events/campaigns 
has been enthusiastic among the foreign communit y, sometimes with the feedback of 
repeating them periodically. These events have generated greater interest and curiosity about 
India and its tourist places. These efforts had a positive impact on tourists‟ arrival from many 
countries to India. For example , 3,69,007 Australian tourists visited India during  the year 

39 
 
2022 which is higher than 3,67,241 Foreign Tourist Arrivals (FTAs) from Australia in the 
year 2019. In comparison to 500 tourist visa and 7 ,779 medical visa  issued by the Indian 
Embassy in Addis Ababa (Ethiopia) from Jan -Dec 2022, Mission has issued 703 tourist visa 
and 10,792 medical visa during Jan-Jun 2023. In Q1 of FY 2023-24, 1789 tourist visas were 
issued in Kuwait, a growth of 30% YOY. Social media out reach by the Missions is getting 
higher impressions and thereby reaching wider audiences. After the closure of overseas 
offices, the Ministry of Tourism in coordination with Indian Missions has participated in 
Arabian Travel Market, Dubai held from 1st to 4th May, 2023 and IMEX, Frankfurt from 23 rd 
to 25th May, 2023. Further details in this regard, are placed at Annexure – XIII.   
148. The Ministry also informed that all possible assistance, including visa facilitation, is 
extended to travel and tour operators/agencies, to enable them to participate in travel expos.   
149. The Committee notes the events and programmes organ ised by the Indian 
Missions abroad to promote tourism and the budgets for the same. The Committee has 
been informed that the Tourism Officers posted in Indian Missions have tourism 
promotion as additional charge . The Committee recommends that as responsibility for 
tourism promotion abroad now rests solely with the Indian Missions in all countries, the 
Tourism Officers may be given sole charge as far as possible as it is necessary for the 
transitional change from the Overseas Tourist Offices to the Missions in many countries 
and to create confidence among the stakeholders that the Missions are attaching top 
priority to tourism promotion . The Tourism Officers were des ignated by MEA in 
October, 2021. The Committee has been informed that since then the Tourism Officers 
have had only one meeting with the Indian stakeholders a year back and desires to 
know how many meetings have been held with them as also the foreign tour ism agents 
and B2B players since their appointment. 
150. Upon enquiry by the Committee on the background of these Tourism Officers 
and whether they have been imparted training in promotion of tourism, it has been 
informed that these officers were already dealin g with  the subject of  promotion of 
Indian culture and commerce and hence were having some domain understanding.  The 
Ministry also informed that as such, the Missions and Posts had been carrying on 
promotional activities wherever there we re no Overseas Tour ism Offices. The 
Committee appreciates the competence of the MEA officers but feels that the MEA may 
explore the possibility of posting officers from the Ministry of Tourism, who are well 
trained and experienced i n tourism promotion,  in Indian Missions ab road. The 
Committee has also been informed that the MEA is considering a proposal to the 
Ministry of Tourism to designate Tourism Officers in all Missions, in addition to the 20 
Tourism Officers already designated in top 20 source markets of tourism and 
recommends that it would be beneficial to have designated officers in more Missions 
abroad for tourism promotion. 
151. The Committee has been informed by the stakeholders tha t after the closure of 
the Overseas Tourist Officers, India did not figure in  the brochures of the overseas 
tourism agents, adversely impacting the prospects of Indian tourism. In its interaction 

40 
 
with the MEA, the Committee has been informed that the Overs eas Tourist Offices had 
a lot of funds available for this purpose and the Ministry of Tourism is still having 
discussions with the MEA on how to place a separate budget for tourism at the disposal 
of the MEA which would enable them to give brochure support  for the agents and other 
promotional work. The Committee is surprised that these aspects of budget for 
promotion and campaign schedule for the Missions have not yet been resolved as the 
Overseas Tourist Offices had gradually been shut down over the last two years and only 
the last eight were closed in March, 2023.  There was sufficient time for the two 
Ministries to have the transition formalities in place. Upon further query, the 
Committee has been informed that the MEA is in consultation with the Ministry of 
Tourism to jointly address such concerns arising out of closure of Overseas India 
Tourism Offices. 
152. The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Tourism may settle the 
matters expeditiously so that the MEA would have both the funds and the programme 
to go ahead with the work relating to  tourism promotion . Exclusion of India from 
tourism brochure is a major issue in the absence of which  the visibility of Indian 
tourism places would be difficult to maintain.  
153. Upon  query by the Committee regarding the institutional linkages between the 
Ministry of Tourism and the MEA for meaningful coordination to ensure that inbound 
and outbound tourists are not put to any inconvenience, it was informed that the MEA 
regularly interacts with the Ministry of Tourism on all tourism related matters.  The 
Joint Secretary (Economic Diplomacy) is represented as the MEA nominee on boards of 
many tourism-related committees formed by the Ministry of Tourism like the National 
Board for Adventure Tourism, Medical Value Travel Servi ces and Wellness Services 
Sectional Committee , India MICE Board , etc. The Ministry of Tourism organises 
various meetings from time to time with the MEA and Tourism Officers designated by 
the MEA in Indian Missions regarding enhancing foreign tourist arriva ls to the country 
and to chalk out action plan for popularizing India as a Favourite Tourist Destination 
for enhanced foreign tourist arrivals. 
154. The Committee recommends that at regular intervals, virtual meetings can be 
conducted between the two Ministries by involving the Missions, and other stakeholders 
like tour operators to understand the gaps in tourism promotion and  to identify actions 
required by each stakeholder. 
 
Visa facilitation by Indian Missions/Posts abroad 
155. In its meeting with the representatives of the Ministry of External Affairs, the 
Committee was informed that the MEA acts as an effective coordinating agency for smooth 
implementation of visa related policies between the MHA and Missions/Posts abroad which  
act as implementing agencies. Indian Visa is being granted under three schemes, details of 
which are explained under: 

41 
 
a) Regular (Paper) Tourist Visa:  Tourist Visa is admissible for an applicant coming to 
India for recreation, sightseeing, casual visit to me et friends or relatives, attending a 
short term yoga programme, voluntary work of short duration , etc. Under this scheme, 
an applicant shall have to submit an application on the on -line system in the standard 
visa application form, and thereafter, has to submit hard copy of the same along with 
the supporting documents either themselves or through post/author ised outsourced 
service provider. 
b) E-Visa: With a view to liberalization of Indian Visa policies and ease of travel, the 
Government of India launched e -Visa or electronic Visa (i.e., Digital Visa) Scheme in 
November, 2014 for foreign nationals applying for visa under five sub-categories 
including e-Tourist Visa can be availed for all the purposes which are covered in 
Regular Tourist Visa. It can be availed from any geographical location by the nationals 
of 166 countries. Under e -Visa Scheme, electronic Travel authorization is normally 
granted within 72 hours via e -mail. E-Visa Scheme is therefore convenient and paper -
less. 
c) Visa-on-Arrival (VOA) can be granted to a Japanese, Republic of Korea and United 
Arab Emirates (UAE ) nationals  who are visiting India for a period not exc eeding 60 
days for business, tourism, conference and medical purposes. In the case of UAE, VOA 
facility will be available only to those UAE nationals who had earlier obtained e -Visa 
or normal paper Visa for India irrespective of whether the pe rson has visi ted India or 
not. Upon enquiry by the Committee on whether Visa -on-Arrival was given on 
reciprocal basis, the representatives of the MEA stated that it was based on some 
assessment by the MHA, the Nodal Ministry. 40 countries give Visa on arrival for 
Indians. However, more emphasis is given on e-Visa as it is much more convenient.  
156. Owing to onset of Covid -19 Pandemic, a ll visas (including e -Visas which were 
admissible to nationals of 171 countries) were suspended  from March, 2020. With continued 
ease i n prevalent COVID situation, Visas were restored in a phased manner. Tourist Visa 
(both regular and e -visa) was fully restored from  March, 2022. In pre-Covid period in 2019, 
almost 48 per cent of the visas issued were e-visa whereas 52 per cent were regular visa.   
157. The Committee was informed that the rules and regulations for Visas are formulated 
by the Ministry of Home Affairs (MHA) as per the Allocation of Business Rules. The e -Visa 
is completely online and its p ortal is maintained by the Bureau of Immigration, MHA. Only 
the regular or Paper visa is issued by the Missions as it is required to be stamped.  
158. Upon enquiry by the Committee whether sluggishness in  issuance of visas by 
the MEA was adversely affecting tourism as alleged by some stakeholders, the 
representatives of the MEA submitted that the MEA responds very quickly to any 
difficulty in the ease of obtaining visas . They further clarified that due to the onset of 
Covid, visa regime  was suspended and then after consultation with the Ministries of 
Home Affairs and Health, the same was gradually restored, first the regular visa in 
certain categories, later e-visa and at last, the tourist visa.  

42 
 
159. On 15 th October, 2021, the Government announced that t o promote tourism, 
the first five lakh foreign tourists will be given visas on gratis basis.  Till 31 st March, 
2022 this was implemented. The Committee accepts the submissions of the MEA but 
cautions that all efforts may be taken for expeditious issuance of visas and to ensure 
that tourists are not put to any avoidable inconvenience in obtaining visas, especially the 
medical visas. 
160. The Committee observes that China issues Stapled Visas to the people of J&K, 
Arunachal Pradesh and Ladakh , etc. but travel under su ch visas is not allowed by the 
Government even in cases of sportsmen selected for various international sporting 
events. The Committee recommends that t he MEA may look into the issue with due 
seriousness in the interest of finding a durable solution to this vexatious problem. 
 
I. OUTSOURCING OF VISA ISSUE FUNCTIONS  
161. Upon enquiry by the Committee, it was informed that the Ministry of External 
Affairs has outsourced front -end visa - related support services in some of the Indian 
Missions/Posts abroad. Outsourced Service Providers (OSPs) are selected on the basis of 
competitive bidding in open tender process. At present, VFS Global is OSP for 27 Indian 
Missions/Posts abroad in 14 countries. VFS Global is OSP for visa -related services for many 
foreign Embassies/Consulates in India. Besides VFS Global, the other OSPs are: BLS 
International Services Ltd., IVS Global Private Ltd. and Indo -German Consultancy Servic es 
(IGCS). 
162. In its meeting with the representatives of the MEA, the Committee had raised 
the matter of the poor quality of services being provided by the Visa Facilitation 
Services (VFS) at New Delhi and enquired about the selection process of such companies 
and the steps taken to ensure that such companies provide seamless travel to tourists, 
both Indian and foreign and their adherence to the laws of the land they are operating 
from. The representatives of the MEA informed that VFS is hired by Missions abro ad 
and the Government is not involved in the process. 
163. The Committee is not satisfied by the reply as the deficient services rendered by 
such companies reflects poorly on India both inside the country and abroad. The 
Committee asked the MEA for a written reply on this issue.  In its written reply, the MEA 
has stated that OSPs are selected by concerned foreign Embassies/Consulates on the basis of 
their own procedures and practices. OSPs including VFS Global are required to comply with 
Indian laws and rules & regulations pertaining to health, unprecedented situations like 
COVID-19 pandemic, finance, labor laws, etc . In case of any breach, necessary action may 
be taken by the concerned Department/authorities against them. With respect to foreign 
tourists seeking Indian visa, it is submitted that at present consular, passport and visa services 
are outsourced in 59 Indian Missions/Posts abroad in 31 countries. Out of this, VFS Global 
provides consular, passport and visa services in 27 Indian Missions/Posts abroad i n 14 
countries. They have to abide by the terms and conditions stipulated in the contracts. These 

43 
 
contracts are framed as per GoI GFRs and other extant instructions and vetted by Legal 
Division of the Ministry. In case of any violation of the contractual p rovisions, there are 
provisions to impose fines and penalties.  
 
164. The Committee reiterates its stand that the poor quality of services rendered by 
visa issuing companies reflects on India‟s image as a tourist destination. The first 
interaction of a foreign tourist before arrival in India lies with the Visa issuing 
authorities and he gets his first impression of the country from that interaction. Poor 
quality of services, serpentine queues, long waiting times, harassment inside the Visa 
office, all portray a negative impression of the destination country. The situation is no 
better for the Indian tourists who are made to run from pillar to  post to be able to get 
the visa . The Committee also disagrees with the MEA‟s view that OSPs are not a 
concern of the Indian Government as they are selected by foreign Embassies in India 
and by Indian Missions abroad. The Committee stresses that in India, all companies are 
obliged to obey Indian laws and the concerned Ministry has to monitor the situation to 
ensure that visa services, both in India and abroad, are being rendered smoothly for it 
substantially affects the inbound tourists.  
* * * * 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
RECOMMENDATIONS/OBSERVATIONS – AT A GLANCE 
 

44 
 
B. NICHE TOURISM AREAS IDENTIFIED BY THE GOVERNMENT  
1. SUSTAINABLE TOURISM  
The Committee notes that India has been ranked at 139, 134, and 128 in the 
years 2015, 2017 and 2019, respectively under Environmental Sustainability in the 
World Travel and Tourism Competitiveness Ranking. While the new National Strat egy 
and National Board for Sustainable Tourism have come into force in 2022, the Ministry 
had been promoting Sustainable Tourism in India for nearly a decade after the launch 
of Sustainable Tourism Criteria for India in 2014. Not much progress has, however , 
been made in development of Sustainable Tourism in the last decade as substantiated by 
India‟s low rankings in this category. The Committee emphasizes that economic 
development should not be at the cost of the environment and recommends that the 
Ministry may ensure that the revised Sustainable Tourism Criteria be scrupulously 
implemented to minimize the adverse impact of tourism on the environment. While a 
Certification programme for tourism stakeholders, who have successfully implemented 
sustainable criteria, has been in existence for nearly a decade, only one Certification has 
been effected till date. The Committee desires to know the number of hotels surveyed 
for the Certification Programme and the strategy adopted for the survey. The 
Committee observes  that while disbursement of monthly newsletters to stakeholders 
and workshops are useful for dissemination of information, the feedback from 
stakeholders like hoteliers and tour operators is equally important to comprehend the 
problems being faced while im plementing policies on the ground level. The Committee 
feels that the pace of the Ministry in promotion of sustainability in tourism is sluggish 
and recommends that the Ministry may step up its coordination efforts with both the 
stakeholders and the other Central Ministries for effective promotional campaigns.   
(Para 19) 
The Committee desires to know about the action taken on formulation of 
dedicated schemes, destination, and product development and other initiatives taken by 
the National Board for Promotion of Sustainable Tourism.     
(Para 20) 
2. RURAL TOURISM 
The Committee is at a loss to note that the 2022 list of the United Nations World 
Tourism Organisation (UNWTO) featuring the 32 best tourism villages from 22 
countries,  does not contain a single village from India while several countries have two 
villages in the list, and Spain having even three villages. The Committee desires to be 
informed about the number of Indian villages which were nominated for the selection 
by the UNWTO.  The Committee notes that Khonoma in Nagaland has been selected for 
the “Upgra de” programme which would enable the village to receive the necessary 
support from the UNWTO to improve its conditions to meet the selection criteria.  The 
Committee wishes to know the efforts being taken for the development of Khonoma 
after its inclusion in the upgrade list of the UNWTO.  The Committee also desires to 

45 
 
know as to how many Indian villages have so far been selected by the UNWTO in its 
best tourism villages list and the impact of such selection on the village tourism.  
(Para 25) 
The Committee observes that in a predominantly agrarian and rural economy 
like India, rural tourism has immense potential for boosting employment and income 
for villagers while offering opportunities to the city dwellers to take a break from the 
hectic pace of urban lif e to experience nature and local culture at a much slower pace. 
India is home to a diverse range of unique villages, each with its own cultural, 
architectural and historical significance. Unique villages such as „Mattur‟ in Karnataka 
where all the residents speak only Sanskrit and many others offer a glimpse into India‟s 
rich traditions and culture. The Committee, however, observes that the Rural Tourism 
website does not provide details as to how to travel to the unique villages nor details 
regarding rural homestays for most of the villages. Even the Khonoma village page does 
not provide any useful details for the tourists. The Committee recommends that the 
Ministry may ensure that the Rural Tourism website has all details of rural homestays, 
travel connecti vity and tourist spots in respect of every village featured for the 
convenience of the tourists.   
(Para 26) 
 The Committee while appreciating the Best Village Competition initiated by the 
Ministry, is dismayed to note that in the past one year, only one M odel Rural Cluster 
has been identified, without any action being taken on the ground. The Committee notes 
that the villages to be covered in the Vibrant Villages programme are located in far -
flung areas, and close to borders, often in difficult terrains. T he Committee feels that 
the State of Uttarakhand has a number of border villages with rich traditions and local 
culture and recommends that the Ministry may make concerted efforts to develop the 
Model Rural Cluster in Uttarakhand within a targeted time schedule.   
(Para 27) 
3. ADVENTURE TOURISM 
 The Committee notes that there have been an increasing number of accidents in 
Adventure sports, especially in paragliding, which has claimed the lives of a number of 
tourists in the last few years. As per media reports, even “soft adventure” sports like 
trekking have led to casualties in certain situations. The Committee has been informed 
by stakeholders that 3 Gs - Guidelines, Guides and Gears – are critical in risk 
mitigation. The Committee has also been informed that the Ministry has released 
guidelines for safety and quality norms for 15 Land Based, 07 Air Based and 07 Water 
Based adventure tourism activities, which cover the entire range of thrilling activities. 
Besides, some States have also adopted various regulatory measures and safety 
protocols to a ddress the inherent concerns of adventure tourism. ISO 21101:2014 and 
BIS have laid down safety management standards for compliance by adventure 
activities service providers that act as benchmarks for safe adventure activities. The 
Committee desires to kno w about the impact of such safety guidelines on reduction of 

46 
 
accidents in adventure tourism. The Ministry may give data on the number of accidents 
as also the casualties which have taken place in adventure tourism in the country in the 
last five years. The Committee has also been informed that while testing and 
certification of  PPE (Personal Protective Equipment) kits is necessary, it is difficult for 
small Indian manufacturing enterprises to obtain the universally accepted international 
certifications lik e UIAA ( French) (International Climbing and Mountaineering 
Federation). The Committee recommends that testing labs where certification is done 
by the BIS may help small enterprises in this field.   
(Para 33) 
The Committee feels that strict adherence to safety guidelines and implementation of 
safety protocols can be possible only if responsible and licensed/ certified adventure 
tour operators handle the activities. Licensed tour operators play an important role  in 
promotion of adventure tourism as they conduct thorough risk assessments, implement 
safety measures, and provide high quality safety gears, besides engaging experienced 
and professional guides to handle emergency situations. The Committee has been 
informed that presently, adventure tour operators‟ licenses are issued by either the 
Centre or the States and there are still a lot of unlicensed tour operators in the 
country‟s adventure tourism sector who operate without requisite permits, certifications 
and regulatory approvals to meet the requirements of safety, quality and also the law in 
the field. The Committee desires to know what action has been taken or is proposed to 
be taken by the Ministry on the unlicensed adventure tour operators. The Committee 
has also been informed that the Ministry has circulated a draft model adventure 
tourism law to all the States/UTs for feedback. The Committee stresses that  high risk 
adventure sports cannot be regulated only by guidelines and recommends that the 
Ministry m ay make all efforts to come out with laws soon so that only licensed and 
responsible operators who adhere to the laid down safety norms remain in business to 
provide adventure tourism services.  
(Para 34) 
 The Committee has been informed that the governmen t run institutions like 
Himalayan Mountaineering Institution in Darjeeling, the Nehru Institution of 
Mountaineering in Uttarkashi are booked two -three years in advance for a course in 
mountaineering or skiing or water sports. There is, therefore, a dearth of well trained 
guides in adventure sports. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should roll 
out more training programmes for guides and explore the possibility of establishing 
more institutions of international standards for adventure sports across the country.  
The Committee also feels that the training institutes are mostly located in North India 
with focus on high altitude mountaineering which is not so relevant for adventure sports 
in South India.  The Committee recommends that at least one or two Training Institutes 
should be established in South India which focus on adventure sports suitable for its 
topography, altitude and climate.   
(Para 35) 

47 
 
 Regarding the standardization of safety gears, the Committee has been 
informed that presently, there is no single system of certification of adventure 
equipments in our country. The Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) is working towards 
setting standards for adventure equipments that can demonstrate proven track record 
of reliability to help ensure a safe ad venture experience. The Committee expresses its 
displeasure that India does not have standardized safety gears even for high risk 
adventure sports and recommends that the Ministry may expeditiously implement a 
uniform standard across the country for all ad venture tourism verticals, prepared by 
Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS).  
(Para 36) 
 The Committee appreciates the initiative of Adventure Tourism Rescue Centre 
of the Ministry to provide for specialized facilities in emergency situations and urges 
that th e Ministry may ensure that medical treatment facilities are available near all 
adventure tourism locations.   
(Para 37) 
The Committee recommends that National Insurance companies may also be 
encouraged to have policies covering adventure tourism for licens ed adventure tour 
operators, adventure guides and the tourists to provide support and promotion of 
adventure tourism.  
(Para 38) 
 The Committee notes that since adventure tours are usually conducted in 
remote areas, adventure tourism has a significant impa ct on economic development by 
way of income generation for local communities, increased business opportunities, and 
job creation. Besides, adventure tourism can empower local communities by involving 
them in tourism-related activities. At the same time, th e Ministry should take proactive 
steps to determine the carrying capacity of popular adventure tourism destinations to 
mitigate potential negative impacts such as environmental degradation and over 
tourism. The Committee observes that the Ministry should p romote sustainable and 
responsible tourism management practices to harness the full development potential of 
adventure tourism.  
(Para 39) 
4. ECOTOURISM 
 The Committee observes that guides and naturalists are one of the most 
important elements in imparting quality ecotourism experience by way of providing 
valuable educational, interpretive and experiential services to the visitors. The 
Committee has been informed that a Certification Scheme for tour operators and other 
service providers for ecotourism and adventure tourism is to be framed by the Ministry. 
The Committee desires to know whether the Scheme has been framed and the number 
of guides/naturalists who have been certified under this scheme so far.   
(Para 45) 

48 
 
5. MEETINGS, INCENTIVES, CONFERENCES, AND EXHIBITIONS (MICE) & 
WEDDING TOURISM 
The Committee notes that India has less than 1% share in the Global MICE 
business despite being one of the fastest growing economies. India was ranked 28th with 
158 meetings in 2019 International Congress and Convention Association (ICCA) 
ranking of countries. Indian cities are ranked poorly in the international ranking of 
cities by ICCA in terms of the number of interna tional associations meetings. There are 
only 07 cities of India in the top 475 cities, the topmost being Delhi at the 75 th position 
with 37 meetings held and Ahmedabad at the bottom at the 424 th position with 04 
meetings. Even Goa, which is one of the coun try‟s top tourist destinations, is ranked at 
the 307 th position with 08 meetings. The Committee also notes that India‟s outbound 
MICE market is one of the fastest growing markets globally.  
(Para 50) 
 The Committee observes that as per the National Strateg y document on MICE, 
India‟s core MICE infrastructure and amenities are at par with most of the developed 
countries. The main reasons for the poor performance of India in MICE tourism is lack 
of focussed approach on MICE tourism, absence of system for captu ring information 
about various national and international MICE events and preparing for winning the 
bids,  high tax structure and costs and lack of standardization of processes and services. 
Besides, most of the successful international cities have their c onvention bureaus to 
market and provide support to the organisations and associations hosting events 
whereas there are no city convention bureaus in India. Singapore, Thailand, UAE, 
China, Macau and Malaysia are already preferred destinations for MICE even ts in the 
region and India faces tough competition from these countries.     
(Para 51) 
The Committee observes that the Convention industry can enhance a city‟s 
reputation and visibility. Creating a marketing body to get more events will help, 
nurture and m arket the MICE investments in the city. The Committee recommends 
that the Ministry may make all efforts to ensure that such MICE Promotion Bureaus 
may be set up in the six identified cities within a targeted time schedule to market and 
showcase the respective destinations as a favourable location for hosting business events 
and activities. 
(Para 53) 
The Committee observes that there are about 80,000, 04 star and 05 star hotel 
rooms in approved hotels, which for a country of the size of India, is quite insig nificant 
(Annexure – II). The total number of rooms in the city of Bangkok is nearly three times 
than that of India. Adequacy of quality hotels can enhance the reputation of a 
destination as a suitable location for hosting MICE events, thus contributing to  
satisfaction of participants, repeat business besides word -of-mouth recommendation. 
Therefore, adequate four and five star categories hotel rooms would have to be 
increased, for realising the goals of MICE tourism. The Committee recommends that 

49 
 
the Ministry may assess the number of Convention Centres in the 04 and 05 star hotels 
of international standards in the country and determine the shortfall that would be 
required to host the targeted MICE events. 
(Para 54) 
 The Committee also observes that connectivity, especially by air, is one of the 
important aspects that promotes MICE tourism. Since good international air 
connectivity reduces travel time and logistical challenges, the Ministry should 
coordinate with the Ministry of Civil Aviation to ensure that the identified cities of 
MICE destinations are well connected for international flights.   
(Para 55) 
6. WEDDING TOURISM 
The Committee has been informed that one crore weddings take place in India 
every year and the  number is bound to increase in the coming years. The Committee 
observes that Destination Weddings with events and celebrations spread out over many 
days can offer several benefits to the local economy and local ecosystem. But, India‟s 
huge potential for wedding tourism has not been exploited.  The Committee appreciates 
that Kerala Tourism has launched a music video, “Shubha Mangalyam” in 
collaboration with a Youtuber and singer, for promotion of wedding tourism in Kerala 
which has garnered much popularity.   The Committee recommends that similar efforts 
may be made by other states to promote destination wedding tourism.  
(Para 57) 
The Committee recommends that apart from development of infrastructure and 
facilities to support the growth of wedding tourism, the Ministry may also facilitate easy 
and uniform process of registration of marriages in every state of India even for couples 
belonging to other states. 
(Para 58) 
7. CRUISE TOURISM 
The Committee observes that prior to Covid, there was a 35 per cent year -on-
year growth of cruise traffic in the country. Post -Covid, from 2021 onwards, there has 
been an increase in cruise traffic but the pre -Covid growth has still not been achieved. 
Prior to Covid, there were 4.7 lakh passengers on cruises and now, about 3 la kh 
passengers have been on cruises.  
(Para 62) 
 The Committee also observes that the Cruise Shipping Policy was first 
formulated by the Ministry in 2008 when the industry was in its infancy in India. The 
Policy aimed, inter -alia, to increase the number of cruise passengers by 10 lakh by the 
year 2010, well developed rail, road and air connectivity and promotion of cruise 
vessels. Customs duty on bunkering for cruise and coastal ships, then at 33%, was cited 
as a factor towards making cruise operations uncom petitive. As part of conducive fiscal 

50 
 
regime measures this issue was resolved by the Department of Revenue vide Notification 
No. 31/2014 dated November 11, 2014 by exempting Customs and Excise duty leviable 
on bunker fuels. Some progress has definitely bee n made in cruise tourism inasmuch as 
India now has its own luxury cruise liners. The first International Cruise Vessel from 
Chennai to Sri Lanka has recently been flagged off in June, 2023. India is, however, still 
way behind achieving the target of 10 lak h passengers as even in 2023, not even half the 
target has been achieved . The statistics on cruise vessels at some of the Major Ports 
provided by the Ministry show that cruise tourism has a long way to go to catch up with 
the pre -Covid numbers. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should 
endeavour to calibrate the strategy for Cruise tourism by addressing the challenges like 
inadequate infrastructure, lack of proper facilities at ports, environmental concerns due 
to waste disposal and pollution, etc. in a time -bound manner, besides giving incentives 
to cruise companies to come to Indian ports.     
(Para 63) 
 The Committee has been informed about five projects sanctioned under the 
Assistance to Central Agencies Scheme for promotion of Cruise Tourism in Kerala 
(Annexure-III) from 2016 -17 to 2019 -20. The Committee desires to kn ow if similar 
assistance has been given for promotion of Cruise Tourism in other States.   
(Para 64) 
8. MEDICAL AND WELLNESS TOURISM 
The Committee observes that lack of regulation on quality of service is a major 
concern and appreciates that the National S trategy is to encourage registration of all 
medical tourism facilitators with the Government. The Committee, however, 
recommends that such registration should be mandatory as standardisation of services 
is the biggest impediment affecting medical tourism i n India. The Committee 
recommends that while NABH accreditation may be highlighted in various source 
markets, the Ministry should encourage major Indian hospitals to obtain the JCI 
accreditation.  
(Para 72) 
The Committee also observes that till now, no sig nificant efforts or investments 
have been made to build an ecosystem around hospitals. Guesthouses and patient 
specific hotels need to be built around hospitals.   
(Para 73) 
The Committee also recommends that the Ministry may explore the 
opportunities in cross border telemedicine as India has sufficient skilled personnel to 
become a global hub in this regard. The Committee also recommends that the Ministry 
may promote the traditional medicines of India like Ayurveda on an equal footing. 
(Para 74) 

51 
 
9. FILM TOURISM 
 The Committee observes that films can have a massive impact on destination 
marketing as visitors are often inspired to visit filming locations featured in popular 
films. The impact is such that the annual tourist influx is said to have increased by  40% 
in New Zealand, the site of the movie trilogy of “Lord of the Rings” after the release of 
the films. In India, the release of the film "3 Idiots" led to a significant increase in 
tourist arrivals in Ladakh to visit the Pangong Lake featured in the film. India produces 
the highest number of films in the world and film tourism has significant potential for 
growth in India. Many Indian films, especially those regarding period dramas, 
showcase historical places and characters and can be useful to highlight  the history 
behind the tourist places. The Tamil movie “Ponniyin Selvan” based on one of the 
greatest novels in Tamil literature, is a fictional account of the historical character of 
Raja Raja Chola, the Chola king who built the Brihadeeswarar Temple in Thanjavur, 
one of the biggest temples of India. The Chola dynasty was famous for building temples, 
many of which are now World Heritage Sites. The Committee notes that the Tamil 
Nadu Tourism Development Corporation has announced a “Chola Tourism Circuit” 
covering the Chola Temples and the Veeranam Lake, also built by the Cholas. Tourism 
potential of such historical and religious sites can be harnessed through the medium of 
films. The Committee feels that while India has the advantage of producing the highes t 
number of films in the world which also in a way depict the innate historical 
significance of the places of tourism interests, the Ministry has not paid adequate 
attention to the great potential of films to showcase India. The Committee recommends 
that the Ministry should endeavour to devise the strategy to fully tap into the benefits of 
film tourism.   
(Para 77) 
10. GOLF TOURISM 
The Committee observes that Golf was introduced to India by the British and in 
1829, the first golfing establishment, the Royal  Calcutta Golf Club, was established. In 
contrast, Thailand‟s first professional golf was established only in 1924. Yet Thailand 
has emerged as the world‟s top golfing destination while golf tourism is yet to take 
shape in India. In fact, many Indians visi t Thailand as golf tourists. The Committee 
observes that India has much of the same landscapes and climatological conditions as 
Thailand, like a sunny climate all year around in most places which makes winter golf 
possible. Yet, India has not taken any spe cial initiative to attract both domestic and 
international golf enthusiasts. Thailand has promoted a Thai Golf Pass which is a 
unique loyalty programme exclusive to Thailand providing a seamless golf experience. 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry s hould come up with initiatives such as 
developing world -class golf infrastructure, promoting tournaments, and providing 
holistic tourism experience to the visiting golfers in the country. 
(Para 80) 

52 
 
 During its Study Tour in Kerala, the Committee had been informed that an 
amount of Rs. 2464.99 lakh had been sanctioned to Sports Authority of India under the 
Scheme “Assistance to Central Agencies for Tourism Infrastructure Development”  in 
Kerala for upg radation of the Golf Course at the 165 year old SAI Trivandrum Golf 
Club under the Sports Authority. The Committee observes that the project was 
sanctioned in 2016 -17 but as per media reports, the upgradation seems to have been 
taken up only in 2020-21. The Committee further observes that only half the sanctioned 
amount has been released and the status of the project is stated to be “Project Closed”.  
The Committee desires to know the reasons behind this when in reality, the project is 
not completed. The Co mmittee also desires to know whether other Golf clubs in the 
country have been given similar assistance under the Scheme.  
(Para 81) 
 The Committee observes that the Ministry is yet to take significant action on 
Golf Tourism, Eco -tourism, Film Tourism, and  Wedding Tourism while taking some 
measures to promote Sustainable Tourism, Rural Tourism, MICE and Medical 
Tourism, and Cruise Tourism. A plethora of wildlife sanctuaries and tiger reserves exist 
in India yet not much attention seems to have been paid to Wildlife Tourism. Kerala has 
shown the way for Caravan Tourism with the establishment of one Caravan Park and 
proposal for 3 new Caravan Parks, 10 Caravans and subsidy of Rs. 7.5 lakhs for 
Caravan owners. Despite the successful efforts of Kerala towards promotion of Caravan 
Tourism, the Ministry has not endeavored to replicate the same in other states. The 
Committee also appreciates the efforts of Kerala Tourism to develop the “Muziris 
Heritage Circuit”, the largest Heritage Conservation Project in India wh ich would 
reinstate the historical and cultural significance of the legendary port of Muziris and 
also the Alappuzha Heritage Circuit which includes the development of the defunct port 
of Alappuzha into a marina.  The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
explore the potential of Casino Tourism, Culinary Tourism, and Polo tourism, the last 
being believed to have originated in Manipur. The Committee also observes that in the 
aftermath of the pandemic “Remote Working” has gained traction. Due to the rise in 
online work, workers often move to locations even in other countries which offer a 
lower cost of living or scenic locations, etc. and continue to stay for a long period of 
time. India offers all modern facilities required for such remote working, like g ood 
internet facilities, a low cost of living compared to Western countries and many serene 
and beautiful places for living. The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
explore the possibilities of such niche tourism experience.  
(Para 82) 
The Committee  further observes that though the Ministry has drafted the 
National Strategies and constituted National Boards for most areas of Niche Tourism, 
developed websites and published newsletters yet, coordination with the concerned 
Ministries and formulation of project schemes under the various niche areas seem to be 
wanting. The pace of implementation on the ground appears sluggish. Most of the 
National Boards and Task Forces have held only 2 -3 meetings in a year. Most of the 

53 
 
websites for Niche Tourism do not pr ovide complete and detailed information, 
necessary for the tourists. The Ministry‟s interactions with the stakeholders and State 
Governments appear sporadic. The growing gap between the strategic vision and 
implementation needs to be bridged and the Commit tee recommends that the Ministry 
may take necessary action for the same. 
(Para 83) 
The Committee observes that while the Ministry‟s tourism policies stress the 
importance of attracting private investment and developing Public Private Partnerships 
for development of tourist infrastructure and facilities, the Ministry has not given any 
information on PPP projects in any scheme, be it niche tourism, thematic circuits or 
destination tourism. The National Strategy on Eco -Tourism has PPP partnerships as 
one of its strategic components. The Committee has come to know from the website of 
the Department of Economic Affairs regarding PPP projects that Eco -tourism projects 
have been proposed in Andaman & Nicobar Islands by Ministry of Home Affairs 
(MHA). The Commi ttee desires to know the status of these projects and whether the 
Ministry of Tourism proposes to develop similar projects under Eco -tourism and other 
niche tourism areas and Swadesh Darshan 2.0 on PPP basis. The Committee directs the 
Ministry to submit de tails of PPP projects taken in the various schemes so far. The 
Committee observes that private participation in product development or marketing 
and promotion in tourism can be valuable and recommends that the Ministry may 
endeavour to bring in private investments in its various tourism products and schemes. 
(Para 84) 
 The Committee observes that most of the tourists in India come from four 
source countries of US, Canada, Bangladesh and UK which account for nearly 60% of 
the inbound tourists. India has only  a small percentage of tourists from Europe, Africa, 
Central and South Americas. The tourist inflow from West Asian countries is also very 
low though the number of outbound tourists from India to these countries is much 
higher. The Ministry has informed th e Committee that improving connectivity with 
other regions especially direct flight connectivity, tapping the potential of medical 
tourism, promoting India as a hub for MICE tourism, especially after the inauguration 
of world -class convention centre at Pra gati Maidan, creating better infrastructure for 
eco-tourism, adventure tourism, religious tourism circuits, emphasizing on cleanliness 
and security, etc. are some of the steps that can be taken to increase inbound tourism in 
India. The Committee acknowledg es that synergy among the various Central Sector 
Schemes of different Ministries is essential for promotion of   tourism. To achieve this, 
inter-Ministerial coordination is of crucial importance. The Committee has been 
informed by the stakeholders that consultations between the Ministry of Tourism, other 
Ministries and the associations take place in a fragmented manner and there is no 
proper coordination and consultations among Ministries involving matters of tourism in 
the country. The Committee recommends  that the Ministry of Tourism may step up 
efforts for coordination with other concerned Ministries for tourism promotion. 
(Para 85) 

54 
 
The Committee stresses that connectivity to places especially to places of niche 
tourism which are often in far flung areas is of utmost importance in the direction of 
promoting tourism in the country. The Committee has been informed by stakeholders 
that even places like Aurangabad, which as part of the Golden Triangle, used to be well 
connected to as many as eight cities is now connected to only two places. The Committee 
desires to know from the Ministry, the reason behind this drastic measure. The 
Committee further recommends that the Ministry may pay special attention to the air 
connectivity of tourist places being promoted as also of the existing popular places of 
tourism interest.   
(Para 86) 
The Committee has been informed by the stakeholders that the present level of 
training and skilling is not adequate as niche tourism demands high level of training 
and skilling to meet the specific requirements and expectations of the tourists pertaining 
to that sector of niche tourism. The Committee recommends that the tourism sector 
needs to undertake reskilling and upscaling of its manpower by way of imparting them 
skill upgradation capacity building training in different niche areas from time to time.  
(Para 87) 
The Committee observes that out of the 1,291 approved hotels in India only 565
