hotels belong to Three Star and below categories and out of the 1,00,980 hotels rooms 
only 18,225 rooms belong to these categories which is grossly inadequate to meet the 
tourism numbers targeted by the Ministry. The Committee also observes that tourists, 
especially in niche tourism, come from all strata of society and the hotel accommodation 
should cater to all categories of tourists. The Committee recommends that the Ministry 
should incentivise those in the hospitality business to make concerted efforts to ensure 
the availability of sufficient number of hotel rooms of all categories for the optimal 
growth of tourism sector. 
(Para 88) 
  The Committee further observes that creation of hotel infrastructure 
would involve a mammoth capital investment.  The Committee has been informed that 
as return on investment in the hospitality sector takes at least 15 years, availability of 
long term loans becomes a problem. The Com mittee recommends that grant of 
infrastructure status to the hotel industry may be examined on priority as loans with 
longer repayment schedule and suitable interest rates would be available for creation of 
hotel infrastructure. The Committee has been informed  that though industry status has 
been accorded to hotels by many state Governments, but the incentives and privileges 
associated with the industry status have not been confer red to the hospitality sector. 
Granting Industry status to the tourism and hospita lity sector would entitle the sector 
across all states to pay various taxes and tariffs on industrial rates instead of  on 
commercial rates presently. The Committee recommends that the Ministry may 
consider grant of Industry status to the tourism and hospit ality sector by way of a 

55 
 
uniform central notification as it would enhance the overall competitiveness of the 
tourism industry.   
(Para 89) 
The Committee has been informed by the stakeholders that Tourist transport 
vehicles are treated on par with trucks an d other commercial vehicles due to which the 
speed limit is restricted for tourist vehicles whereas private cars have higher speed 
limits and faster movement. In areas of niche tourism like MICE and luxury tourism, 
high-end tourists do not enjoy the rides especially in long road journeys and as such, the 
speed restrictions cause avoidable annoyance in the tourist experience. The Committee 
has learnt from the media reports that the Government is planning to review the speed 
limits and recommends that the Tou rist transport vehicles may not be treated in the 
same category as other commercial vehicles and the speed limits may suitably be 
revised/increased. Another minor but significant point raised by the stakeholders was 
that curtains should be allowed in tourist buses as tourists, especially the foreigners, are 
not used to tropical weather, especially the heat of Indian summers. The Committee 
observes that tourism promotion is as much about the wholesome journey experience as 
it is about the destinations and recommends that the Ministry may consider the issue.   
(Para 90) 
The Committee has been informed by the stakeholders that high tax rates in 
India in comparison to neighbouring countries (Annexure – III) is affecting tourists‟ 
arrival in India. The Committee observes that in 2019, the GST rates had been reduced 
for hotel room from 28% to 18% for hotel rooms with tariffs of Rs. 7500/ - and above, 
18% to 12% for tariffs of less than Rs. 7500/ - and none for tariffs of less than Rs. 1000.  
The Committee appreciates  the initiative of the Ministry of Finance and recommends 
that the Ministry of Tourism may examine the impact of the 2019 reduction in GST on 
tourist arrivals and appropriately take up with the Ministry of Finance for further 
reduction in GST, if need be, for the targeted growth of tourism sector.   
(Para 91) 
 The Committee observes that under the Service Exports from India Scheme 
(SEIS), the Government offered domestic exports duty credit scrips in the range of 5% 
to 7% of the net foreign exchange earned d epending upon the nature of services. These 
SEIS scrips could be used to pay import duty or can be encashed by selling it to any 
importer. The SEIS Scheme had not been extended beyond 2019 -20. While examining 
the Demand for Grants 2023 -24, the Committee ha d recommended that the Ministry 
take up the SEIS issue with the Ministry of Commerce to increase the rate of SEIS 
benefits to 10% and extend the scheme for a further period of 2-3 years. The Committee 
reiterates its earlier recommendation as even after 02 years after the pandemic, the 
tourism sector is yet to redeem its pre-pandemic position.  
(Para 92) 
 

56 
 
11. SPIRITUAL TOURISM  
The Committee notes that of 23 Spiritual Circuits, 05 are yet to be completed out 
of which 02 circuits of Kerala are lagging much behind schedule. The work on the 
Sabarimala-Erumeli-Pampa-Sannidhanam circuit in Pathanamthitta district in Kerala 
had been initiated in 2016 -17 while the Sivagiri Sree Narayana Guru Ashram circuit 
had been initiated in 2018 -19. At the time of the Demands for Grants 2023 -24, the 
Committee had been informed that the reason for delays in these two Kerala Circuits 
was due to lack of clearance from the Temple Authorities. The Committee has been 
informed during its Study Visit to Kerala in September, 2023 that t he status of physical 
completion of the two projects is 76% and 51% , respectively. The Committee is 
disappointed to see the slow progress on these important projects which have a target 
completion date of June, 2023 and October, 2023 respectively . The Comm ittee 
recommends that the Ministry should develop a clear cut Standard Operating 
Procedure (SOP) and obtain prior clearances and approvals from relevant authorities 
for future compliance in the interest of timely completion of projects.  
(Para 95) 
The Committee notes that the Buddhist circuit has been fully developed but as 
can be seen from the Tourism Statistics 2022, the numbers of foreign tourists from 
predominantly Buddhist countries has not shown any significant uptick. The Committee 
recommends that th e Ministry may focus on promotion of Buddhist Circuit in these 
countries and desires to know the number of events conducted by the Indian Missions 
there, showcasing India‟s Buddhist circuit. The Committee recommends Audio -Visual 
Guides that offer informati on about Buddhist sites and monuments are trained by a 
proper agency. 
(Para 96) 
The Committee has been informed that Bodh Gaya, one of the most important 
sites in the Buddhist circuit, does not have good connectivity with the rest of the places 
in the Budd hist circuit. The Committee calls upon the Ministry to examine the 
connectivity issue of Bodh Gaya and focus on improving the overall connectivity of all 
the Spiritual Circuits for increasing tourist footfall. 
(Para 97) 
C. PRASHAD SCHEME  
The Committee observes that at the time of the Demands for Grants, 2023 -24, 
the Committee had been informed that 20 out of 45 projects under this scheme had been 
completed, 09 projects were ongoing and 16 projects were yet to be developed.  The 
Committee observes that the status remains almost the same as only 21 projects have 
been completed. Most of the reasons for delay given by the Ministry earlier were due to 
lack of approvals from the ASI, temple authorities, forest clearance, etc.  
(Para 99) 

57 
 
The Commi ttee observes that the PRASHAD scheme was launched in 2014 -15 
and completion of 21 projects out of 45 is certainly not a significant achievement, even 
taking into account the Covid years. The Committee points out that spiritual circuit 
projects were taken under Swadesh Darshan Scheme 1.0 also and the Ministry has had 
adequate experience of the kind of problems that ordinarily come up while dealing with 
construction and development at pilgrimage sites. The Committee calls upon the 
Ministry to be mindful of t he potential problems and calibrate suitable response at the 
DPR stage, so that the projects are not held up at later stages, causing both time and 
cost overrun.   
(Para 100) 
The Committee appreciates that the Ministry has taken the initiative to amend 
guidelines and funding pattern midterm where necessary and recommends that the 
Ministry may revise the timeline of the delayed projects and ensure that the projects 
under this scheme are completed within the revised timeline. The Committee also 
recommends that the PRASHAD Dashboard when online may be monitored on a real 
time basis at the level of a senior functionary in the Ministry to ensure the completion of 
all pending projects.  
(Para 102) 
E. PROMOTION OF SWADESH DARSHAN SCHEMES 
The Ministry of Tourism pro motes tourist destinations including theme -based 
tourist The Committee notes that at the time of the Demand for Grants 2023 -24, the 
Ministry had informed that 17 projects of Swadesh Darshan 1.0 were pending for 
completion. Four more projects have been comp leted and now 13 projects are targeted 
for completion by December, 2023. The Committee notes that out of the remaining 13 
projects, only 04 are showing some progress and the rest are still at the same stage as 
they were in March, 2023. The Committee furthe r notes that 02 projects are delayed 
due to court case/arbitration, 04 due to lack of clearance from forest/temple authorities 
and the remaining 03 due to delays in procurement of cruise boats and floating 
restaurants, contractor issues and inclement weath er and difficult terrain (in case of 
J&K). The Committee feels that the lack of progress in these projects in the past few 
months does not augur well for their timely completion by December, 2023. The 
Committee also notes that most of these projects began in 2016 -17 and are already 
much behind their schedule. The Committee also notes that the four projects which are 
delayed due to lack of clearances from forest or temple authorities are at the final stage 
of completion. The Committee desires to know why the  clearances have not been 
obtained at the time of the finalisation of the DPR and are pending even at the 
culmination of the project. The Committee recommends the Ministry to make every 
effort to complete the remaining 13 projects by December 2023 as the p rojects have 
already been considerably delayed. The Committee also desires the Ministry to monitor 
the real time progress of the projects at the level of a senior functionary and ensure 
their completion without any further delay.  

58 
 
(Para 114) 
The Committee appreciates the Utsav Portal website and the Tourist Train 
initiatives of the Ministry. The Committee recommends that the Ministry should study 
the impact of the projects completed under the Swadesh Darshan 1.0 scheme on the 
tourism and analyse the shortcomings so that the same could be improved in SD 2.0.   
(Para 115) 
F. SWADESH DARSHAN 2.0 
When enquired by the Committee on the criteria of selection of 55 destinations 
under Swadesh Darshan Scheme 2.0, the Ministry informed that the States had been 
consulted on the same and a format had been circulated to the States for preparing the 
State Perspect ive Plan. The Ministry further informed that there are two principles 
involved in selection of a destination that states which have not been represented should 
find a place in the list and places with immense potential should also be included . The 
Committee recommended that a wide consultation process should be initiated before the 
selection of any such destinations as they have a great impact on the local economy. 
(Para 120) 
Upon observation of the Committee that 50 destinations had also been 
announced in the Union budget speech, 2023, the Ministry confirmed that these 50 
destinations were over and above the 55 destinations under the Swadesh Darshan 2.0 
and would be selected in challenge mode. Both the State Governments and the 
Stakeholders at the Chintan S hivir meeting had been asked for feedback and the 
selection process would be fairly competitive. The Committee emphasised that selection 
of destinations should be a consultative process so that there is an equitable distribution 
of destinations in the country across sectors.   
(Para 121) 
The Committee observes the differences (Annexure – VIII) in funding pattern, 
disbursal of funds and receipt of Utilisation Certificates in the Swadesh Darshan 2.0 
Scheme, and appreciates the new features of Impact Assessmen t of the projects under 
the Scheme and monitoring of the Scheme through a Comprehensive Portal. The 
Committee, however, observes that all the themes of the Swadesh Darshan 1.0 Scheme 
have been repeated in the 2.0 Scheme with no new additions. The claim of the Ministry 
to focus on fewer destinations in the second phase of the Scheme has also been diluted 
by the addition of 50 more destinations.   
(Para 122) 
The Committee observes that there does not appear to be a clear cut distinction 
between the two phases of the Swadesh Darshan Scheme as the same theme based 
strategy seems to have guided the implementation of the second phase also. The 
Committee is also not sa tisfied with the reply of the Ministry that all themes evolved in 
future will fall in one of the major themes as the basic idea of niche tourism is that it 
provides a particular experience which cannot be obtained in other forms of tourism.  

59 
 
Regarding benchmarks and standards, the Committee fails to understand why the same 
were not provided in the earlier phase of the scheme. The Committee feels that the 
Ministry has in the past decade alternated between theme -based tourism and 
destination based tourism and  has endeavoured to incorporate elements of both in all 
the programmes that it has formulated. The Committee recommends that the Ministry 
may ensure that the guidelines issued to the states are unambiguous in the kind of 
destinations and themes to be developed. 
(Para 124) 
The Committee appreciates the initiatives taken by the Ministry to prepare a 
skilled workforce for catering to the increasing demands of the tourism industry for its 
anticipated growth.  The Committee desires to know the number of students  in the 
tourism courses at IITTM, the number of trained guides and whether the numbers 
would be adequate to meet future demand.  
(Para 129) 
The Committee appreciates that the Ministry has taken the digital initiative of 
the Incredible India Tourist Facilitator (IITF) Online Programme to enable people to 
learn about tourism at their pace, earn Certificates as Facilitators, and become part of 
the tourism industry. The Committee has been informed that the Facilitators certified 
under the IITF scheme are  not allowed to enter the monuments as a result of which 
their employment prospects are affected. Tour Operators hesitate to employ Facilitators 
as they would also have to separately employ guides to go into the Monuments with the 
tourists. The Committee d esires to be informed of the status in this regard and 
recommends that the Tourist Facilitators may be imparted training in all aspects of 
tourism so that they may act as Guides inside the monuments as well. The Committee 
also observes that in places like Serengeti National Park in Tanzania, the drivers of the 
Safari Vehicles are well trained to act as guides to the Park. The Committee 
recommends that the Ministry may explore the possibility of multi -skilling of the 
Tourist Facilitators so that they may als o perform the roles of Drivers and Guides, 
depending on the requirements.  
(Para 130) 
G. POTENTIAL TOURIST SPOTS 
In the meetings with the stakeholders, the Committee was informed about 
various potential tourist spots in the country which have been placed a t Annexure – IX. 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry may examine the concept of Greenfield 
Tourism where new locations can be developed as potential tourism spots with the 
participation of Government of India and the concerned State Government with private 
investments for creation of modern tourist infrastructure, hotels and all tourist facilities 
and connectivity by air, rail and road. The Statue of Unity near Kevadia in Gujarat is a 
classic example of Greenfield Tourism, being a man -made tourist destination which has 
earned a formidable reputation as a popular tourism spot. Another example is tourism 
on the sand dunes near the Salasar Balaji Temple in the Churu District of Rajasthan 

60 
 
where hotels and restaurants have come up to cater to the tourists who visit the temple 
and stay to enjoy the sand dunes in the desert. The Committee observes that 
development of such Greenfield tourist spots would take the pressure off the already 
established tourist spots which sometimes suffer from the adverse effects of 
overtourism.     
(Para 134) 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry may while promoting destinations 
in Arunachal Pradesh or Lakshadweep or such areas which require special permits, 
highlight the requirement in its campaigns so that tourists are aware of the rules and 
are able to plan their schedule accordingly.  
(Para 135 
 The Committee further observes that the Ministry had formulated the Draft 
National Tourism Policy in July, 2022 incorporating key recommendations of NITI 
Aayog, Sectoral Group of Secretaries, global best practices from organizations such as 
UNWTO and polic ies and strategies formulated by other countries after multiple 
rounds of consultations and feedback from Central Ministries, State Goverments and 
Industry Stakeholders.  More than a year later, the policy, however, has still not been 
finalized.  The Commi ttee recommends that the Ministry may make every effort to 
ensure that all concerns of the stakeholders in the tourism and hospitality sector are 
addressed and the National Tourism Policy is put in place in a time-bound manner.  
(Para 136) 
H. PROMOTION OF TOURISM ABROAD BY INDIAN MISSIONS 
The Committee observes that there were 10.93 million Foreign Tourist Arrivals 
(FTAs) in India during pre -pandemic year 2019, but the current FTA numbers are still 
well below the pre -pandemic levels. In one of the studies b y the National Council of 
Applied Economic Research (NCAER), it is estimated that tourism in India would not 
regain its pre -pandemic levels till 2026. In this scenario, promotion and marketing of 
tourist destinations and products, especially by the Indian Missions abroad, assumes 
importance if India is to regain its pre-pandemic tourism numbers.  
(Para 146) 
The Committee notes the events and programmes organised by the Indian 
Missions abroad to promote tourism and the budgets for the same. The Committee has  
been informed that the Tourism Officers posted in Indian Missions have tourism 
promotion as additional charge. The Committee recommends that as responsibility for 
tourism promotion abroad now rests solely with the Indian Missions in all countries, the 
Tourism Officers may be given sole charge as far as possible as it is necessary for the 
transitional change from the Overseas Tourist Offices to the Missions in many countries 
and to create confidence among the stakeholders that the Missions are attaching top  
priority to tourism promotion. The Tourism Officers were designated by MEA in 
October, 2021. The Committee has been informed that since then the Tourism Officers 

61 
 
have had only one meeting with the Indian stakeholders a year back and desires to 
know how ma ny meetings have been held with them as also the foreign tourism agents 
and B2B players since their appointment. 
(Para 149) 
Upon enquiry by the Committee on the background of these Tourism Officers 
and whether they have been imparted training in promotion of tourism, it has been 
informed that these officers were already dealing with the subject of promotion of 
Indian culture and commerce and hence were having some domain understanding. The 
Ministry also informed that as such, the Missions and Posts had been  carrying on 
promotional activities wherever there were no Overseas Tourism Offices. The 
Committee appreciates the competence of the MEA officers but feels that the MEA may 
explore the possibility of posting officers from the Ministry of Tourism, who are w ell 
trained and experienced in tourism promotion, in Indian Missions abroad. The 
Committee has also been informed that the MEA is considering a proposal to the 
Ministry of Tourism to designate Tourism Officers in all Missions, in addition to the 20 
Tourism Officers already designated in top 20 source markets of tourism and 
recommends that it would be beneficial to have designated officers in more Missions 
abroad for tourism promotion. 
(Para 150) 
The Committee has been informed by the stakeholders that after the closure of 
the Overseas Tourist Officers, India did not figure in the brochures of the overseas 
tourism agents, adversely impacting the prospects of Indian tourism. In its interaction 
with the MEA, the Committee has been informed that the Overseas Tourist Offices had 
a lot of funds available for this purpose and the Ministry of Tourism is still having 
discussions with the MEA on how to place a separate budget for tourism at the disposal 
of the MEA which would enable them to give brochure support for the agents and other 
promotional work. The Committee is surprised that these aspects of budget for 
promotion and campaign schedule for the Missions have not yet been resolved as the 
Overseas Tourist Offices had gradually been shut down over the last two years and only 
the last eight were closed in March, 2023.  There was sufficient time for the two 
Ministries to have the transition formalities in place. Upon further query, the 
Committee has been in formed that the MEA is in consultation with the Ministry of 
Tourism to jointly address such concerns arising out of closure of Overseas India 
Tourism Offices. 
(Para 151) 
The Committee recommends that the Ministry of Tourism may settle the matters 
expeditiously so that the MEA would have both the funds and the programme to go 
ahead with the work relating to tourism promotion. Exclusion of India from tourism 
brochure is a major issue in the absence of which the visibility of Indian tourism places 
would be difficult to maintain.  
(Para 152) 

62 
 
Upon  query by the Committee regarding the institutional linkages between the 
Ministry of Tourism and the MEA for meaningful coordination to ensure that inbound 
and outbound tourists are not put to any inconvenience, it was informed that the MEA 
regularly interacts with the Ministry of Tourism on all tourism related matters. The 
Joint Secretary (Economic Diplomacy) is represented as the MEA nominee on boards of 
many tourism-related committees formed by the Ministry of Tourism  like the National 
Board for Adventure Tourism, Medical Value Travel Services and Wellness Services 
Sectional Committee , India MICE Board, etc. The Ministry of Tourism organises 
various meetings from time to time with the MEA and Tourism Officers designate d by 
the MEA in Indian Missions regarding enhancing foreign tourist arrivals to the country 
and to chalk out action plan for popularizing India as a Favourite Tourist Destination 
for enhanced foreign tourist arrivals. 
(Para 153) 
The Committee recommends that at regular intervals, virtual meetings can be 
conducted between the two Ministries by involving the Missions, and other stakeholders 
like tour operators to understand the gaps in tourism promotion and to identify actions 
required by each stakeholder. 
(Para 154) 
Visa facilitation by Indian Missions/Posts abroad 
Upon enquiry by the Committee whether sluggishness in issuance of visas by the 
MEA was adversely affecting tourism as alleged by some stakeholders, the 
representatives of the MEA submitted that the MEA responds very quickly to any 
difficulty in the ease of  obtaining visas. They further clarified that due to the onset of 
Covid, visa regime was suspended and then after consultation with the Ministries of 
Home Affairs and Health, the same was gradually restored, first the regular visa in 
certain categories, later e-visa and at last, the tourist visa.  
(Para 158) 
On 15th October, 2021, the Government announced that to promote tourism, the 
first five lakh foreign tourists will be given visas on gratis basis. Till 31 st March, 2022 
this was implemented. The Committee accepts the submissions of the MEA but cautions 
that all efforts may be taken for expeditious issuance of visas and to ensure that tourists 
are not put to any avoidable inconvenience in obtaining visas, especially the medical 
visas. 
(Para 159) 
The Committee observes that China issues Stapled Visas to the people of J&K, 
Arunachal Pradesh and Ladakh, etc. but travel under such visas is not allowed by the 
Government even in cases of sportsmen selected for various international sporting 
events. The Comm ittee recommends that the MEA may look into the issue with due 
seriousness in the interest of finding a durable solution to this vexatious problem. 
(Para 160) 

63 
 
I. OUTSOURCING OF VISA ISSUE FUNCTIONS  
In its meeting with the representatives of the MEA, the Committee had raised the 
matter of the poor quality of services being provided by the Visa Facilitation Services 
(VFS) at New Delhi and enquired about the selection process of such companies and the 
steps taken to ensure that such companies provide seamless  travel to tourists, both 
Indian and foreign and their adherence to the laws of the land they are operating from. 
The representatives of the MEA informed that VFS is hired by Missions abroad and the 
Government is not involved in the process. 
(Para 162) 
The Committee is not satisfied by the reply as the deficient services rendered by 
such companies reflects poorly on India both inside the country and abroad. The 
Committee asked the MEA for a written reply on this issue. 
(Para 163) 
The Committee reiterates it s stand that the poor quality of services rendered by 
visa issuing companies reflects on India‟s image as a tourist destination. The first 
interaction of a foreign tourist before arrival in India lies with the Visa issuing 
authorities and he gets his first  impression of the country from that interaction. Poor 
quality of services, serpentine queues, long waiting times, harassment inside the Visa 
office, all portray a negative impression of the destination country. The situation is no 
better for the Indian to urists who are made to run from pillar to post to be able to get 
the visa. The Committee also disagrees with the MEA‟s view that OSPs are not a 
concern of the Indian Government as they are selected by foreign Embassies in India 
and by Indian Missions abroad. The Committee stresses that in India, all companies are 
obliged to obey Indian laws and the concerned Ministry has to monitor the situation to 
ensure that visa services, both in India and abroad, are being rendered smoothly for it 
substantially affects the inbound tourists.  
(Para 164)

99 
 
Annexure – IX 
 
ANALYSIS OF THE DIFFERENCES BETWEEN SWADESH DARSHAN 1.0 AND 2.0 
S.No. Descriptio
n 
Swadesh Darshan 1 Swadesh Darshan 2.0 
1 Themes (i) Culture and Heritage  
(ii) Wildlife-Tourism 
(iii) Rural Tourism  
(iv) Beach Tourism  
 
(i) Culture and Heritage  
(ii)  Adventure Tourism 
(iii)  Eco-Tourism 
(iv)  Wellness Tourism  
(v) MICE Tourism  
(vi) Rural Tourism  
(vii) Beach Tourism  
(viii) Cruises – Ocean & Inland 
2 Vision and 
mission 
 Thematic Circuit approach Destination and Tourist Centric 
approach 
 - To develop sustainable and 
responsible tourism destinations in 
the country. 
3 Disbursal 
of Funds 
30% of the project cost on 
submission of work orders 
 
30% of the project cost after 
receipt of utilization certificate 
for the first installment 
 
 25% of the project cost after 
receipt of utilization certificate 
for the second installment 
 
 10% of the project cost on 
completion of the work and 
receipt of utilization certificate 
 
5% of the project cost after 
successful Operations & 
Maintenance ( O&M) of 
Toilet, TFC, Cafeteria facilities 
for one year as certified by an 
independent agency 
On sanction of project (mobilization 
advance) -10% of the sanction cost. 
 
On su bmission of work orders/ 
agreements -30% of the tendered cost 
(after adjusting mobilization advance) 
 
 35% of the tendered cost upon 
Utilization of minimum 100% of the 
1st & 75% of the 2nd installment - 
 
 30% of the tendered cost upon 
Utilization of 100% of 1 st & 2 nd and 
minimum 75% of the 3rd installment. 
 
5% of the tendered cost upon Physical 
completion of the project : Utilization 
of 100% of 1st, 2nd &  3rd and 
minimum 75% of the 4th installment, 
Certificate of 100% physical 
completion of the project from 
Implementing Agency countersigned 
by Administrative Secretary.  
 
O&M costs as per the tendered cost to 
be paid annually Successful 
completion of annual  O&M of the 
project as certified by an independent 
agency. (3 years) 
 

100 
 
4 Funding 
Pattern 
The Scheme is 100% centrally 
funded; 
 
1. Other schemes of Central 
and State Governments and 
also to leverage the voluntary 
funding available for 
Corporate Social 
Responsibility (CSR) 
initiatives of Central Public 
Sector Undertakings and 
Corporate Sector. 
 
2. Suitable Public Private 
Partnerships to be taken up for 
improved sustainability of the 
projects.  
 
3.  A Special Purpose Vehicle 
(SPV) may be created for the 
purpose, wherever feasible.    
 
4. The funds would be released 
to the Implementing Agency, 
under Swadesh Darshan 
Scheme. The funds shall be 
sanctioned strictly in 
conformity with the  
GFRs and the directions issued 
by the M/O Finance from time 
to time. All the proposals will 
be routed through IFD of M/O 
Tourism.  
 
5. Funds would be released 
only after submission of copy 
of work orders and good for 
construction DPR by the 
implementing ag ency to the 
Ministry. 
6.  Upto 10% funds shall be 
earmarked for IEC components 
(like workshops, seminars, 
publications, stakeholder 
outreach, skill development , 
etc.) & Professional & 
Administrative Services. 
The Scheme 100% Centrally Funded; 
 
Funds for Pr ofessional and 
Administrative services  
1.  3% funds for Professional, 
Administrative and Office Expenses 
by MOT. The fund will be util ised at 
the National Mission Directorate level 
for : 
(i) Hiring of National Program 
Management Unit  
(ii) Fees for Central Technical Nodal 
Agency  
(iii) Administrative expenditure on 
Mission Directorate activities 
including national and regional 
review meetings, workshops and field 
visits, etc.  
(iv) Capacity building through 
National Resource Centres/COE  for 
various themes identified under the 
Scheme: 
 (a) IEC material for capacity building  
(b) National & regional workshops 
for capacity building 
 (c) Giving awards and recognition of 
best practices 
 (d) Up-scaling and replication of best 
practices and smart solutions  
(e) Research and applied studies  
(f) International cooperation for 
capacity building and technology 
development  
(v) Setting up IT portal  
(vi) The Apex Committee will decide 
on the use of these funds for any other 
purpose. 
 
2. 5% funds for Professional, 
Administrative and Office Expenses - 
State Level/ Implementing Agency 
The amount will be util ised by the 
States/Implementing Agencies for the 
following purpose:  
(i) Hiring of Project Development and 
Supervision Consultants, other 
professionals and support teams on 
contract to support the 
implementation of Mission at 
State/destination levels will be 

101 
 
permissible as set out in the 
guidelines and after following fair and 
transparent procedures.  
(ii) Institutional arrangements that 
support Missio n implementation will 
be eligible to be funded.  
(iii) It shall not be used for purchase 
of vehicles, construction and 
maintenance of buildings, creation of 
posts, payment of salary of existing 
staff and purchase of furniture and 
fixtures, etc. 
5 Utilisation 
Certificate 
To be submitted at the time of 
release of first, second and 
third instalments 
The implementing agency shall 
submit the Utilization Certificate of 
the funds released as follows: 
(i) The UC shall be as per Form 12 -C 
of General Financial Rules-2017.  
(ii) The UC shall be verified by 
Technical/ Engineering Head of 
the Agency for compliance with 
technical/ engineering codal 
formalities. 
(iii) The UC shall be verified by 
Finance & Accounts Head of the 
Agency for compliance with 
financial and accounting c odal 
formalities. 
(iv) The UC should thereafter be 
countersigned by Administrative 
Head of the Agency.  
(v) The UC signed by Implementing 
Agency shall then be 
countersigned by Administrative 
Secretary In-charge of Department 
of Tourism of the State 
Government and s hould be 
forwarded with a forwarding letter 
from the State Department of 
Tourism to the Ministry of 
Tourism. 
(vi) Relevant provisions under GFR & 
related amendments/directions 
issued by the Ministry of Finance, 
Government of India from time to 
time towards util ization 
certificates & refund of unspent 
amount/interest, etc. shall be 
applicable. 
6 Impact 
Assessme
-  (i) Increase in tourist footfall, 
duration of stay and spend at the 

102 
 
nt destination 
 
 (ii) Employment generation at the 
destination 
 
(iii) Enhancement of skills of local 
youth in tourism and hospitality  
 
(iv) Private sector investment at the 
destination 
7 Monitorin
g 
1. Ministry of Tourism would 
periodically monitor the 
scheme through designated 
officers.  
2. Both online & offline mode  
monitoring would be done. 
 3. Implementing agencies 
shall submit the updated 
Monitoring Framework by 5 th 
of every month.  
4. Third party service audit 
would be done for WSA/Toilet 
blocks for regular monitoring 
purposes. 
1. Setting up of Portal for the Scheme. 
- A comprehensive portal on Swadesh 
Darshan Scheme should be set up, 
which will serve the Central, State 
and district authorities to monitor the 
progress of the scheme and various 
objectives. All the processes of the 
scheme will be digit ised and wi ll be 
executed through the portal. It will 
provide the requisite management and 
analytical reports and a dashboard. 
All proceedings of the review 
meetings at various levels will also be 
uploaded on the portal.  
 
2 Monthly Progress Report: Monthly 
progress report (MPR) shall be 
submitted by the State/UT 
Implementation agency on the 5 th of 
every month online on the portal. All 
proceedings of the review meetings 
will also be uploaded on the portal.

distributed.

distributed.

MINUTES OF THE MEETING OF THE DEPARTMENT-RELATED 
PARLIAMENTARY STANDING COMMITTEE ON TRANSPORT, TOURISM & 
CULTURE (2022-23) 
XXVI 
TWENTY-SIXTH MEETING 
  
The Committee met at 03:00 P.M. on Monday, the 26 th June, 2023 in Committee Room -
4, First Floor, Parliament House Annexe Extension, New Delhi. 
Members present: 
1.    Shri V. Vijayasai Reddy - Chairman   
Rajya Sabha 
2. Shrimati S. Phangnon Konyak

WITNESSES 
(i) ******* 
 
Ministry of Tourism 

141 
 
(i) Ms. V. Vidyavathi, Secretary, Ministry of Tourism 
    (Complete list of witnesses is attached as per Annexure-I)  
  
2. The Chairman welcomed the Members of the Committee and informed them that the 
agenda of the meeting was to hear the representatives of ******* and the second item on the 
agenda is to hear the Secretary, Ministry of Tourism on the subject 'Development of Niche 
Tourism (including Spiritual Tourism), Theme-based Tourist Circuits and Potential Tourist 
Spots. 
3.        ***** 
(After the representatives of ****** took their seats) 
4.        ******* 
5.        ******* 
6.  *******  
7.  ******* 
8. ******* 
9. *******1 
(The witnesses then withdrew) 
(After the Secretary, Ministry of Tourism and other officials took their seats) 
10. The Chairman welcomed the Secretary, Ministry of Tourism. He then remarked that 
despite India being a 365 -day tourist destination offering a diverse array of products for Niche 
Tourism as well as various Theme -based Tourist Circuits and diverse geographica l features, the 
tourism potential still remains untapped. He queried about the reasons why India has been unable 
to fully exploit its potential for Niche Tourism as compared to its smaller neighbours, namely, 
Thailand, Nepal, and Bhutan who are achieving t remendous success in becoming the top golf, 
mountain, and sustainable tourism destinations respectively. He further queried about the areas 
identified for the development of Niche Tourism in the country, the number of Niche Tourism 
projects under implement ation, particularly in three important categories of Tourism - Medical, 
MICE, and Spiritual Tourism, challenges faced in developing a destination as a Niche Tourism 
hub and the coordination mechanisms employed by the Ministry with various State 
Governments/ Departments/ agencies. The Chairman asked the Ministry to apprise the 
Committee of the number of projects completed and those which are under development under 
the Theme-based Tourist Circuits identified under the ‘Swadesh Darshan Scheme (SDS) Phase -
1’ along with their physical and financial progress and the time and cost overruns, the status of 
Spiritual Circuit projects and further plans for their development.   He also enquired into the 
reasons for the SDS moving away from Theme -based tourist circuits and focusing on revamping 
Destination Tourism, potential tourist spots identified under the ‘SDS -2.0’ and the criteria 
                                                           
1 Relates to other matters 

142 
 
employed for selection, steps taken for the welfare of local communities and creation of 
employment opportunities in the areas identified for the Scheme. 
11. The Chairman then remarked that many States like Assam, Gujarat, Kerala, Rajasthan, 
Punjab, Goa, Karnataka, Uttarakhand, Maharashtra, Jammu & Kashmir, Himachal Pradesh and 
Tripura have granted 'industry' status to tourism, which provide s benefits such as electricity 
tariff, lower local taxes, availability of land on subsidized rates, etc. But the hotel and resort 
industry being capital-intensive require huge investments and has long gestation periods.   Hence, 
the Ministry should take up the issue with the State/Central Government to grant the hotel and 
resort industry the ‘infrastructure’ status to enable it to avail longer credit terms, greater 
investments, and better access to commercial borrowings as well as tax benefits/concessions. H e 
also queried about the reason for the delay in the implementation of the Draft National Tourism 
Policy which was released in July, 2022.  
12. Through her presentation, the Secretary, Ministry of Tourism informed the Committee 
that the Ministry is current ly promoting ten Niche Tourism Products, namely, Sustainable 
Tourism, Rural Tourism, Adventure Tourism, Eco -tourism, Meetings Incentives Conferences & 
Exhibitions (MICE), and Wedding Tourism, Cruise Tourism, Medical & Wellness, Film Tourism 
and Golf Touris m and Spiritual Tourism.   The Ministry informed the Committee of the steps 
taken for each Niche product, viz. formulation of National strategies in consultation with State 
Governments and Industry, guidelines and framework for each Niche tourism product, and 
launch of Travel for LIFE Programme under Sustainable Tourism. The Ministry has also 
launched the Best Tour ism Village Competition to identify the potential areas which received 
751 applications from Rural Tourism Villages.   E-Conference Visa was also granted for the 
MICE events.  Also, the Ministry has launched a new Incredible India film on MICE Tourism 
and is preparing for a much bigger digital campaign for MICE Tourism.   
13. The Ministry further informed that it is jointly working with other Central Ministries on 
the Niche tourism areas which fall under their jurisdiction.   It is working with (i) the Minist ry of 
Environment, Forest & Climate Change for   Eco-Tourism, (ii) the Ministry of Shipping, Ports, 
and Waterways, for developing coastal areas/destinations under Cruise Tourism, (iii)   the M/o 
Health and Family Welfare Medical to promote Wellness Tourism a nd (iv) the Ministry of 
Information & Broadcasting for Film Tourism.   
14. The Committee was informed that State Governments are spearheading the promotion 
and marketing of various Spiritual Tourism destinations to attract both domestic and 
international visitors.   The Ministry is also promoting various tourism destinations through its 3 
Schemes, namely, Swadesh Darshan Scheme (SDS), PRASHAD, and Central Financial 
Assistance for Central Agencies.   Under the SDS, 15 thematic circuits have been identified an d 
76 projects have been  sanctioned to date. Out of this 23 sanctioned projects worth Rs. 1365.01 
Crore belong to Spiritual Circuit, including Krishna Circuit, Buddhist Circuit, Spiritual Circuit, 
Ramayana Circuit, and Tirthankara Circuit. The revamped SDS 2.0 has 55 destinations identified 
across 32 States/UTs for development. The Committee was also informed of various successful 
projects of the Ministry under the SDS 1.0 in the States of Odisha, Madhya Pradesh, Puducherry, 
Tripura, Uttarakhand, and Vadnagar (Gujarat). 
15. The Committee was informed that PRASHAD, which was launched in 2015 to create 
infrastructure for pilgrimage/spiritual tourism, has been allocated Rs. 1630.05 Crores for 46 

143 
 
sanctioned projects. Apart from its own website and the Incredible India website under the 
digital initiatives for tourism promotion, the Ministry has also set up the ‘Utsav Portal’ to 
provide live streaming of various destinations and their festivals. The Committee was further 
informed that the Ministry is promoting pote ntial tourist spots through its website as well as 
organizing tourism -related National Festivals such as ‘Bharat Parv,’ and ‘Paryatan Parv,’ 
conducting online and social media campaigns.  
16. The Ministry further informed that it is working on identifying 10 to 12 tourist 
destinations for which it has created a value chain focusing on tourism mapping. It is also 
looking into incentivizing ‘Green tourism’, which has been one of the major points of discussion 
in G-20 meetings. In the G-20 Tourism Working Group, the Ministry created the Goa Road Map 
giving recommendations for Sustainable and Responsible Tourism, best digitization practices of 
various countries, skill development, and tourism Micro, Small, and Medium Enterprises 
(MSMEs). 
17. A Member commented o n the frequent changes in the Swadesh Darshan Scheme from a 
circuit to a single location and the lack of institutional memory regarding past issues and 
decisions. He also raised issues regarding the lack of an inclusive consultation process in 
deciding the  55 tourist spots and equitable distribution of tourist spots in various States.   The 
Secretary informed that 50 new destinations which were also announced in the Budget 2023, will 
be selected in challenge mode and are in an advanced stage of finalization.  The principles 
governing the selection process are the inclusion of states which have not been represented so far 
while keeping a balance between such states and other states.   
18. Another Member queried about the status of the request made by the State Government 
of Andhra Pradesh to develop Gandikota village situated in the Kadapa district of Andhra 
Pradesh, which is also known as the 'Grand Canyon of India,' as a heritage site. In reply, 
Secretary, M/o Tourism informed that Gandikota incidentally was a part of the 'Adopt a Heritage' 
Scheme which was given to the Dalmia Bharat Group, the status of which will be given to the 
Committee. 
19. A Member enquired about the lack of development of Sitamarhi the birthplace of Sita in 
Bihar.  He was informed that a  concept proposal has been requested from the State Government 
and the same was awaited.       
20. Members also queried about the procedure and time taken to issue a visa for MICE, 
Medical and Wellness Tourism, steps taken to relax the norms of obtaining a  medical visa, the 
details of leading countries with the highest number of medical tourists visiting India and the list 
of 156 countries for which e-visa has been restored by Ministry of Home Affairs. A Member also 
queried about the details of sectors and clusters identified for development under Rural Tourism 
and the steps taken to address the language barrier in promoting the same. Members also raised 
various queries regarding:- 
 Lack of basic amenities, such as sanitation facilities, clean water, healthcare services, and 
tourist-friendly infrastructure, such as well-placed signboards, transportation and parking 
facilities, tourist information and helpline centers at tourist destinations and lack of 
coordination among State and Central Governments; 
 Lack of medical facilities at the adventure tourism destinations; 

144 
 
 Need for development of coastal connectivity infrastructure such as “Roll On-Roll Off" 
(RoRo) ferry services around the 16,00 km coastline of the State of Gujarat under Cruise 
Tourism; 
 Developing a tourism destination providing Ayurveda-inspired wellness cuisine of 
various Indian States; 
 Promotion of the State of Manipur as the birthplace of modern polo; 
 Allocating special funds and devising a special master plan to explore responsible 
tourism in the State of Kerala; 
 Challenges and issues faced in obtaining permission from the Forest Department 
regarding carrying out adventure tourism activities such as river rafting, kayaking etc. in 
the forests; 
 Promoting Dolphin watching points at the Ganga River flowing between Patna to 
Bhagalpur in Bihar as a tourism destination; and 
 Developing a very strong Tourism Rating System in India to curb manipulation.  
 
21. The Ministry responded to some of the queries raised by the Members and took note of 
the suggestions made by the Members. The Chairman directed that written replies to the points 
which had not been answered and to the questions forwarded by him may be sent within a week. 
 (The witnesses then withdrew) 
22.      A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
23.      The meeting adjourned at 6:12 PM. 
  
  
New Delhi,                                                                                                           Swarabji B. 
26th June, 2023                                                                                                               Director

MINUTES OF THE MEETING OF THE DEPARTMENT-RELATED 
PARLIAMENTARY STANDING COMMITTEE ON TRANSPORT, TOURISM & 
CULTURE (2022-23) 
XXVIII 
TWENTY-EIGHTH MEETING 
  
The Committee met at 03:00 P.M. on Tuesday, the 18 th July, 2023 in Committee Room -
D, Ground Floor, Parliament House Annexe, New Delhi. 
 
Members present: 
1. Shri V. Vijayasai Reddy - Chairman   
Rajya Sabha 
2. Shrimati S. Phangnon Konyak 
3. Shri Jugalsinh Lokhandwala 
4. Dr. C.M. Ramesh 
Lok Sabha 
5. Shri Tapir Gao 
6. Shri Rahul Kaswan 
7. Shri Chhedi Paswan 
8. Shri Prince Raj 
9. Shri Rajiv Pratap Rudy 
10. Shri Krupal Balaji Tumane 
  
SECRETARIAT 
Shri Sumant Narain, Joint Secretary  
Shrimati Monica Baa, Additional Director  
Shrimati Subha Chandrashekar, Deputy Secretary  
Shri Ranajit Chakraborty, Under Secretary 
  
Shri Kaustubh Sudhakar Bhalekar, OSD to Hon’ble Chairman 
  
WITNESSES 
Federation of Hotel and Restaurant Association of India (FHRAI) 
i. Mr. Pradeep Shetty, Vice-President  
Indian Association of Tour Operators (IATO) 
i. Mr. Rajiv Mehra, President  
Indian Tourist Transporters Association (ITTA) 
i. Mr. Amritpal Singh Mann, President 

147 
 
 
Adventure Tour Operators Association of India (ATOAI) 
i. Mr. Ajeet Bajaj, President 
(Complete list of witnesses is attached as per Annexure-1)  
 
The Chairman welcomed the Members of the Committee and congratulated Smt. 
Phangnon Konyak on her nomination by Hon'ble Chairman, Rajya Sabha to the panel of Vice -
Chairmen.  He further informed the Members that the agenda of the meeting was to hear the 
representatives of Federation of Hotel and Restaurant Association of India (FHRAI), Indian 
Association of Tour Operators (IATO), Indian Tourist Transporters Association (ITTA) and 
Adventure Tour Operators Association of India (ATOAI) on the subject 'Development o f Niche 
Tourism (including Spiritual Tourism), Theme -based Tourist Circuits and Potential Tourist 
Spots. 
(After the representatives of FHRAI, IATO, ITTA and ATOAI took their seats) 
 
2. The Chairman welcomed the representatives of the FHRAI, IATO, ITTA and ATOAI 
and asked them to share their valuable suggestions/ perspectives on  the following aspects of the 
subject under examination:- 
 Impact of the 'Swadesh Darshan’ and PRASAD Schemes on Tourism in general and 
Spiritual Tourism in particular 
 Adequacy of infrastructure, connectivity of important tourist places and safety issues 
 General tourist experience in India as compared to other countries 
 Problems and challenges faced by tourists, especially foreign tourists, and suggestions to 
resolve the challenges and further development of the tourism industry 
 Marketing challenges of Niche Tourism  
 Safety measures taken in adventure sports such as paragliding and river rafting and 
whether such measures meet global safety standards 
 Measures taken by tour operators to ensure safety of women tourists 
 Post-pandemic outlook for both Mass Tourism and Niche Tourism and whether the 
tourism policy responses to the pandemic have been adequate to ensure full recovery of 
the industry 
 Views on the digital initiative - ‘Incredible India Tourist Facilitator (IITF) Certification 
Programme’ of the Ministry of Tourism 
 Coordination mechanism employed by the four Associations with the Government and 
other tourism agencies and stakeholders to promote Indian tourism and the challenges 
faced in this regard 

148 
 
 Suggestions for better coordination between the Government and important stakeholders 
3. The Vice President of FHRAI, through his presentation, informed the Committee that 
FHRAI is an   apex body of all hotels and restaurants in India, re presenting about 55,000 hotels 
and 5,00,000 restaurants and cooperating with the Government for the promotion of tourism. He 
informed that Foreign Tourist Arrivals (FTAs) were around 6.19 million in 2022 as compared to 
about 10 million in 2019 before the pandemic. He also stated that India is at 54 th rank globally in 
terms of FTA Arrivals while countries much smaller than India have had comparatively better 
FTA numbers. The Vice President acknowledged the initiative of the Ministry of Tourism in 
addressing the problem of lack of data and information on availability of hotel/guest rooms for 
tourists in India by starting an initiative to collect data on the number of hotel rooms available 
for tourists in India.  He also expressed his appreciation of the effort s of the Ministry to promote 
film tourism especially in Kashmir.  
 
4. The Vice -President, FHRAI, highlighted some locations mostly in North East India 
which could be considered for promotion under Niche Tourism.   He cited the connectivity 
problems in North -East India where only 3 Airports out of the existing 15 had International 
Arrivals and road transport posed a big problem and Inner Line Permit issues in some places in 
the North -East and Andaman islands due to which some tourists faced difficulties. The Vice 
President further stated that even famous locations like Aurangabad are facing connectivity 
problems. Though five years ago, the city was connected to eight destinations, now it is 
connected to only two. The Buddhist circuit is not well connected to B odh Gaya, one of the key 
areas. He however acknowledged that the Dekho Apna Desh campaign had given a boost to 
domestic tourism.  
 
5. Under Niche Tourism areas, he suggested that art tourism could be promoted by holding 
book and dance festivals like the Ka la Ghoda festival in Mumbai which attracts a lot of 
foreigners, casino tourism, and development of the coastline of South of Maharashtra as a beach 
destination. He also suggested that as the hotel industry requires long -term lending, the industry 
may be included in the Infrastructure Pipeline of the Government, which may solve the problems 
of availability of funds in the industry.  
 
6. The President, IATO informed that IATO is an apex body of the tourism industry 
founded in 1982 and has over 1,700 members f rom different segments of the tourism industry.   
He reiterated the areas of potential Niche Tourism already stated by the Vice -President, FHRAI 
and added that Destination Wedding is a potential area which could be developed in India if the 
Government facilitates formalities like registration of marriage in different states. Regarding the 

149 
 
Swadesh Darshan and other schemes of the Ministry, he stated that though infrastructure had 
been developed, lack of promotion of the places made such development futile whi le citing 
examples in Manipur and Arunachal Pradesh. He also suggested that all inbound tourism 
destinations should be connected with the UDAN Scheme.   
 
7. The President, IATO  drew the attention of the Committee to the fact that no overseas 
promotional activities have been initiated by the Ministry. He also alleged that the promotion of 
overseas tourism was an additional charge given to the Tourism Officers a ppointed in the 
Embassies and the officers were not trained in tourism promotion. To a remark of the Chairman 
that the Overseas Tourist offices were closed because they did not prove to be very effective, the 
President, IATO stated that the offices had als o been promoting India with the B2B people and 
putting India on tourism brochures.   
 
8. Upon being queried by a Member, all the representatives of the various Associations 
affirmed that none of them had been called by the Ministry on international promoti onal 
programmes. The Committee was also informed that the Tourist Facilitators accredited under the 
Certification Programme of the Ministry did not have the permission to enter the ASI 
monuments and hence could not be utilised as guides.             
 
9. The President, IATO also alleged lack of coordination among Ministries which affects 
policy formulations.  He cited the example of the speed restrictions on tourist cars and curtains in 
Volvo buses both of which cause inconvenience to foreign tourists.   
 
10. The Chairman stated that Cabinet notes on proposals of Ministries are circulated among 
the concerned Ministries and that Inter -Ministerial coordination and consultation is a continuous 
process. He enquired whether the Associations wanted a separate counc il apart from the 
consultation process already in existence between the Ministries. The President, IATO replied 
that the National Advisory Tourism Council was already operational at the local level. His 
suggestion was for the formation of a National Touris m Board. He also stated that an incentive 
scheme from the Ministry of Commerce for tourism and hospitality industry called SEIS and a 
Marketing Development Assistance Scheme from Ministry of Tourism which have been 
withdrawn should be implemented as soon as possible.   
 
11. Upon query from the Chairman regarding the impact of the SEIS scheme, it was 
informed that the incentives were being used for promotion of India abroad and its withdrawal 

150 
 
was hindering the tour operators from traveling abroad for promotional activities. Si nce the 
pandemic has posed many challenges for the tourism industry, the incentives should be restored.  
12. The President, Adventure Tour Operators Association (ATOAI), said that during the G20 
Summit in Siliguri and Darjeeling, adventure tourism was the focus of the Tourism Working 
Group. The President, ATOAI acknowledged that risk mitigation was the biggest concern in 
adventure tourism. Licenses can be given by either the Centre or the States but Many operators 
were unlicensed or had substandard equipmen t. Three Gs were critical in risk mitigation - 
Guidelines, Guides and Gears. More guides need to be trained as the guides of the government 
run institutions are booked years in advance. The Bureau of Indian Standards (BIS) is 
standardizing the safety equipm ents in adventure tourism. He also raised the issues of provision 
of insurance coverage to licensed adventure tour operators, conducting study to determine the 
carrying capacity of tourist places and providing air connectivity Nubra and Spiti Valleys and 
open areas in Arunachal Pradesh for Adventure tourism and developing Vibrant Villages as 
destinations for soft adventure and nature tourism. 
 
13. The President, ITTA, stated that transport is the backbone of any country  but India 
however is the only countr y where   all vehicles are called Transport vehicles and there is no 
segregation of tourist vehicles. The speed limit of tourist vehicles are also fixed at 80 kph which 
annoys foreigners who travel in Mercedes and other high end cars.  In the last four years, Benz or 
BMW did not sell even a single luxury ‘S’ Class or BMW’s 7 Series car respectively to tour 
operators because they don’t have speed governors fitted in their vehicles. Stopping of vehicles 
on the pretext of checking papers which also happens in I ndia creates annoyance in tourists. 
Governments of Goa and Karnataka have specially issued notifications that vehicles cannot be 
stopped unless there is clear violation of traffic rules and this should be done pan India.  He also 
commented that the vehicle  capacity in India should be increased as in the past four years there 
have been no additions of vehicles in India, especially in terms of vans or luxury vehicles. 
 
14. The President, ITTA, also stated that since drivers are the backbone of the travel indu stry 
their welfare should be kept in mind. Government of Tamil Nadu has for instance issued a 
circular that hotels should have a dormitory for drivers. The President, IATO said that it was a 
development control rule which has to be discussed. The Chairman observed that such 
expenditure would be treated as overhead. Both Operating expenses and Overhead can be 
clubbed for fixing tariff. The President, IATO, agreed it could be done prospectively.   
 
15. A Member opined that India should initiate a Greenfield policy for Tourism Development 
which would involve declaring a specified area and giving incentives by the Central Government 

151 
 
for development of modern infrastructure, hotels, and all kinds of tourism facilities for potential 
tourism.  The Statute of Unity is a classic example of a Greenfield Tourism destination.  
16. A Member enquired about the meetings conducted by the four associations, and was 
informed that Federation of Associations in Indian Tourism & Hospitality (FAITH) is the 
umbrella body representing the four associations and its meetings happen every two months.  
 
17. A Member enquired about Identification and promotion of unexplored scenic places in 
the state of Nagaland,  whether digital technology, AI can be adopted to provide information to 
tourists and about the coordination meetings of the industry players with the Ministry.   Another 
Member suggested that adventure tourism and religious tourism could be promoted in North 
Eastern states. He also suggested that capital subsidy for hotels in the  North East should be 
resumed so that North East region could be developed for hotels and tourism.   
 
18. A Member opined that tourism had developed rapidly in the past decades and invited 
suggestions from the Associations about the new places in which nic he tourism could be 
developed especially in Yoga and Ayurveda. He also emphasized the need for a national portal 
on cuisine and culinary and Haats on highways like Delhi Haat so that a tourist while travelling 
in different parts of the country knows about the best restaurants and dhabas in those areas. 
 
19. Members also raised various queries and discussed matters such as: 
 Developing Adventure tourism and rural tourism in Rajasthan. 
 Skill training institutes for drivers. 
 GI tags for weaving and different crafts. 
 Connecting tourism to conference facilities viz., Sharm-El-Sheikh city in Egypt, which 
focuses on hosting conferences and events. 
 Concept of driver-cum-guide like in Serengeti, Tanzania.  
 
20. The representatives of the four Associations responded to some of the queries raised by 
the Members and took note of the suggestions made by the Members. The Chairman directed 
that written replies to the points which had not been answered and to the questions forwarded by 
him may be sent within a week. 
 (The witnesses then withdrew) 
21.      A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
22.      The meeting adjourned at 06:09 PM. 
  
New Delhi,                                                                                            Monica Baa

WITNESSES 
1. Shri Prabhat Kumar, Special Secretary (Economic Relations), Ministry of External Affairs. 
(Complete list of witnesses is attached as Annexure) 
 At the outset, the Chairman welcomed the Members of the Committee to the meeting.   He 
further apprised the Members that Sh. Vinay Dinu Tendulkar, a Member of the Committee would 
retire from the membership of the Rajya Sabha on 28 th July, 2023 and placed on record his seminal 
contribution to Committee proceedings. The Chairman, thereafter, welcomed Shri Prabhat Kumar, 
Special Secretary, the Ministry of External Affairs (MEA), and other senior officials of the Ministry 
and asked them to inform the Committee about the "Process of Issua nce of Visa for Inbound 
Tourism" and the "Overseas Promotion of Inbound Tourism" by the Embassie s.  The Chairman 
further enquired whether the Embassies and Missions abroad have successfully supplanted the 
Overseas Tourism Offices in promoting tourism after the closure of the Overseas Offices in March, 
2023. The Chairman noted that the country -wise inbound tourists show that the USA, Canada, UK 
and Bangladesh are the top source countries accounting for nearly 60% of inbound tourists and 
asked about the efforts being taken by the Ministry to increase the tourist inflow from other regions. 
The Chairman also asked for the details regarding efforts made by the MEA towards a lenient visa 
regime and rationalization of Visa/e-Tourist Visa fee.  
  
2. The representative of the Ministry of External Affairs stated that one of main tasks of the 
Ministry was to prom ote Brand India, relating to commercial tourism, abroad and the Ministry had 
already been performing this task in countries where there had been no Overseas Tourist Offices.   
After the closure of the Overseas Tourist Offices in March, 2023, 20 Officers working in the Indian 
Missions abroad, mostly as Commercial and Cultural Officers, have been deputed to look after 
tourism promotion. Responding to the  query of the Committee, the Secretary acknowledged that 
there was no separate posting of Tourism Officers a nd no selection criteria in place. B ut, as the 
officers had been dealing with commercial and cultural matters  in which tourism content formed an 
integral part, they had the requisite experience to deal with  the matters related to tourism promotion 
also.  
 
3. The representative of the MEA, thereafter, detailed the sources of funds for tourism 
promotion, namely the budget for Market Expansion Activities, the State Facilitation Budget used in 
case of promotion of State specific festivals or for delegations fro m a State, publicity budget from 
the MEA and the budget received from the Ministry of Culture for cultural activities.  Upon query of 
the Committee regarding the alleged d eletion of India ’s name  from tourist brochures, as brought 

155 
 
forth by the tour operators, the Secretary replied that the Overseas Tourist Offices used to have a lot 
of funds for brochure support and  promotional campaign, but such budgetary support  was presently 
not available with the Missions.  The MEA was in discussion with the Ministry of Tourism (MoT) 
on these issues. The Missions , however, were actively campaigning i n Social Media  about the 
tourism promotion.  The representative also described some of the promotional campaigns done by 
Indian Missions abroad like road shows, Know India Seminars, “Days of India” promotions, etc.  He 
also informed that the MEA integrated tourism in all its activities including making it  an integral 
part of the outreach programme.   
 
4. Regarding tourist visas, the representative of the MEA informs the Com mittee that while the 
rules and regulations concerning Visas remained under the purview of the Ministry of Home Affairs 
(MHA), the implementation part  fell under the MEA: Of  the three kinds of visas,   issuance of  
regular tourist visa is provided by the Emb assy, e-visa’s provided by  the Bureau of Immigration, 
Ministry of Home Affairs and ‘Visa o n Arrival, ’ which is at present given to citizens of three 
countries, Japan, Republic of Korea and United Arab Emirates.   When the Committee enquired 
about the increase or decrease in e -visas, post Covid,  the representative of the MEA could not 
present the current status of e -visa as the MHA, not the MEA is the  custodian of information in this  
regard.   
 
5. On a query by the Committee whether the slow proces s of issuing e -visas had decreased the 
foreign tourist arrivals to India, the representative responded that based on the correct and  complete 
documentation could be granted, e-visas within 72 hours and added that as a matter of fact, the MEA 
immediately responds to any difficulty in getting visas.   He also clarified that the visa regime had 
been suspended during Covid period and was restored gradually depending on the health conditions, 
first the regular visa in some categories, then the e-visa and lastly the tourist visa. 
  
6. Upon enquiry by the Committee whether the visa on arrival was bas ed on reciprocal 
arrangements, the representative of MEA said that there were 40 countries offering visa on arrival to 
the Indians whereas India grants the same facilit y to only three countries. Thus, it was not on 
reciprocal basis. Instead, e-visa is being promoted, as it is  more convenient than Visa on Arrival. 
The Chairman enquired about the reason for entrusting the power to issue e -visa with the MHA and 
was informed that as per the Business Allocation Rules, the subject of visas falls under the purview 
of the MHA.   Earlier, only regular visas were issued which had to be stamped on passports at the 
Missions abroad and that is why the MEA was tasked with the implement ation of visas. Now, the 
number of e -visas has increased to about 52% of total visas issued because of the convenience of 
applications. One of the  Members appreciated that in Covid times, the e -visa facility was utilised 

156 
 
well to bring back Indians from other countries.  He also appreciated the passport services being 
provided in Ahmadabad, Gujarat. One of the  Members suggested that all the concerned Ministr ies, 
the Ministry of Tourism, the Ministry of Home Affairs and the Ministry of External Affairs should 
discuss and come up with a consolidated action plan with regard to issuance of Visa. 
 
7. A Member enquired about the storage of the 105 antiquities retri eved from USA and was 
informed that they were presently in the Consulate General, New York but as the Ministry of 
Culture was the custodian of antiquities; a team of ASI officers would take over the documentation 
process and ensure return of antiquities to India.   Another Member raised the concerns of tourists, 
and sportspersons, from Arunachal Pradesh, Jammu & Kashmir and Ladakh not being allowed to 
visit China on the basis of stapled visa issued by China.   The representatives of the MEA assured 
that a written reply would be furnished to the Committee on the issue.  
 
8. Another Member raised the issue of preachers  from the Christian community being denied 
visa for religious conventions in Kerala and was informed that there is a separate category for people 
coming for religious activities and if the documentation is complete and visa is applied under the 
right category, clearance is given  without any delay.  A Member also pointed out that many foreign 
tourists coming for Ayurvedic treatment in Kerala face delays in getting visas , as a result their 
medical appointments get cancelled.  Kerala also has many tourist places which depend on tourism 
for inco me and delay s in granting visas , adversely affects the inbound tourism as also the local 
economy. 
 
9. A Member brought up the recent trend of “Remote Working” from anywhere around the 
globe and “Destination Weddings” and asked the Ministry if any initiatives have been taken for the 
encashing on such niche tourism possibilities. He also informed that the so ftware for the “Uber” 
brand had been made in Varkala, Kerala by persons while on ‘remote work’. A  Member enquired 
about the work done by the Ministry in the Incredible India campaign post 2019.   He asked for data 
on the social media outreach programmes, participation in Expos and promotional materials used.  
 
10. The Chairman raised the issue of poor quality of services rendered by Visa Facilitation 
Services (VFS) office at New Delhi and enquired whether the Ministry had selected the company on 
competitive bidding or on nomination basis. The Ministry responded that VFS was hired on 
competitive basis by the respective Embassies and the Ministry was not involved in the process. The 
Chairman was not satisfied with the reply and reiterated that the Company had been found to be 
grossly deficient in providing quality services and said that the company must  obey India n laws 
while operating in India. He directed that the Ministry may submit a written reply in this regard.   

157 
 
 
11. The Chairman also asked about the institutional linkage between the Ministries of Tourism 
and External Affairs to promote tourism and the alleged exclusion of India from the Tourism 
brochures.  
 
12. Members raised other queries as detailed below:- 
 Whether pre-Covid numbers had been reached in medical tourism.  
 Process of patenting basmati rice, turmeric, yoga and Ayurveda by World Intellectual 
Property Organisation and the role of India’s Permanent Mission in Geneva. 
 Possibility of posting well trained Tourism Officers from the Ministry of Tourism in 
Embassies and Missions abroad. 
 Promotion of Health tourism, yoga and Kalaripayattu. 
 Stoppage of border trade through Pangsau pass which is affecting Arunachal Pradesh and 
Myanmar. 
 Problems of accommodation of people coming over the border in U.P. and Gujarat with legal 
visas. 
 
13. Thereafter, the Chairman thanked the representatives of the MEA for their inputs and 
directed that written replies to all the q uestions raised by the Hon. Members and to the three 
questions raised by him may be sent within one week's time.  
 
(The witnesses then withdrew) 
14. A verbatim record of the proceedings of the meeting was kept. 
 
15. The meeting adjourned at 5:00 PM. 
 
 
New Delhi,                                                                                      Monica Baa 
26th July, 2023                                                                                Additional Director
